7: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
7: Letter of Transmittal
7: Board of Education
7: November 14, 2024
7: November 14, 2024
7: Members of the Board of Managers and Citizens of the Houston Independent School District:
7: The Annual Comprehensive Financial Report (ACFR) of the Houston Independent School District (the “District”
7: or “HISD”) for the fiscal year ended June 30, 2024 is hereby submitted. This document is management’s report of
7: financial operations to the Board of Managers (the “Board”), taxpayers, grantor agencies, employees, the Texas
7: Education Agency, and other interested parties.
7: The Texas Education Code requires that all school districts file an annual report with the Texas Education Agency
7: (TEA) within 150 days of the close of each fiscal year. The annual report must be prepared in accordance with
7: generally accepted accounting principles (GAAP) as applicable to governmental entities and must be audited by a
7: firm of independent licensed certified public accountants. ThisAnnual Comprehensive Financial Report is submitted
7: in fulfillment of that requirement.An audit was conducted in accordance with GovernmentAuditing Standards issued
7: by the Comptroller General of the United States and the requirements of Title 2 U.S. Code of Federal Regulations
7: Part 200, Uniform Administrative Requirements, Cost Principles, and Audit Requirements for Federal Awards
7: (Uniform Guidance). The financial statements of the District have been audited by Weaver and Tidwell, L.L.P.
7: To the best of our knowledge and belief, the enclosed information is accurate in all material respects and is
7: reported in a manner designed to fairly present the financial position as well as the financial condition of the District.
7: Disclosures necessary to enable the reader to gain understanding of such financial operations have been included.
7: Responsibility for the accuracy and completeness of the data presented, as well as the presentation of this report,
7: rests with District management.
7: GAAP requires that management provide a narrative introduction, overview, and analysis to accompany the basic
7: financial statements in the form of Management’s Discussion and Analysis (MD&A). This letter of transmittal is
7: designed to complement the MD&A and should be read in conjunction with it. The District’s MD&A can be found
7: immediately following the report of the independent auditors.
7: PROFILE OF THE HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
7: The District was created and incorporated by an act of the Thirty-Eighth Legislature of the State of Texas in 1923.
7: As required by House Bill 1842, passed in 2015 by the Texas legislature, Texas Education Commissioner Mike
7: Morath appointed the nine-member Board of Managers to temporarily replace the elected board of trustees on
7: June 1, 2023. The Board of Managers has the same authority as the elected trustees and will be responsible for
7: overseeing the management of the school district. Based on legislative authority codified in the Texas Education
7: Code, the Board (1) has exclusive power and duty to govern and oversee the District; (2) can acquire and hold real
7: and personal property, sue and be sued, and hold all rights and titles to school property; (3) shall have power to levy
7: and collect taxes and to issue bonds; (4) can contract for appointed officers, teachers, and other personnel as well
7: as for goods and services; and (5) has the right of eminent domain to acquire real property.
7: HISD, with an enrollment of 184,109 students, is the eighth-largest public school system in the nation and the
7: largest in Texas. The District encompasses 333 square miles within Harris County, Texas including 51% of the
7: current geographic area of the City of Houston and all or part of four other cities or villages. HISD is an ethnically
7: diverse district as 61.8 percent of all students are Hispanic, 21.4 percent are African American, 9.8 percent are
7: White, 5.0 percent are Asian and 2.0 percent are of other ethnicities.
8: - 3 -
8: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
8: Houston Independent School District provides services through 274 schools, of which 8 are early childhood centers
8: (pre-kindergarten for four-year-old students), 159 elementary schools (grades K-5), 39 middle schools (grades 6-8),
8: 37 high schools (grades 9-12) and 31 combination/alternative sites. The average age of the District’s schools is 45
8: years. A list of school buildings with year of construction can be found in the Statistical Data Section. (See pages
8: 131-152.)
8: Four division superintendents oversee the elementary, middle, high school and alternative/charter schools. Four
8: senior executive directors, twelve executive directors, and twelve unit leaders report to the division superintendents.
8: These individuals provide leadership to principals, align resources and support for teachers, and ensure that the
8: district is providing equitable and quality educational opportunities to students. HISD’s organization is designed to
8: emphasize teaching and learning, align school goals and programs for sustained improvement, eliminate duplicated
8: services, and provide greater oversight of data and compliance with state laws and regulations.
8: The District serves its diverse student population through an intensive core curriculum and specialized instructional
8: andcareerprograms.TheDistrictprovidesafullrangeofprogramsandservicestoitsstudents,includingelementary
8: and secondary courses for general, vocational, and college preparatory levels, as well as magnet, and gifted and
8: talented programs.Academic programs are supplemented by a variety of co-curricular and extracurricular activities.
8: In addition to the regular educational programs, the District offers programs in special education, bilingual, and
8: compensatory education. HISD contracted for the operation of 7 external performance (charter) schools which are
8: funded with state funds that flow through the District.
8: ECONOMIC OUTLOOK
8: Houston is the largest city in Texas and the fourth most populous city in the United States with a population of 2.3
8: million, and 7.5 million people in the Houston metro area. The region’s largest industries are energy, aerospace and
8: aviation, life sciences and biotechnology, and transportation and logistics. Employment in the Houston Metropolitan
8: Statistical Area stood at 3.732 million people in June 2024 per the Texas Workforce Commission, up three percent
8: from one year earlier. Houston’s economy continues to recover following the pandemic. Per the Greater Houston
8: Partnership, Houston’s economy is performing well. Exports from the port of Houston are rising, construction has
8: picked up, airport traffic is setting records, and home prices have stabilized.
8: LONG-TERM FINANCIAL PLANNING
8: The District is subject to the state’s target revenue formula for school district funding. Under the allocation
8: methodology, the District’s maintenance and operations revenue from property taxes and state aid is determined
8: by a state formula based on weighted average daily attendance. As collections from property taxes increase, state
8: aid is reduced. The District is challenged with providing salary increases and other inflationary fixed costs within
8: this allocation methodology. The District is constantly adjusting future projections as costs in the market shift and as
8: changes are proposed or adopted by the Texas Legislature.
8: Enrollment decreased from the 2022-23 school year by 5,825 students. The 2024-25 budget includes an additional
8: reduction of 4,011 students. As of October 2024, the District’s certified net taxable value for the 2024 tax year
8: increased by 1.0 percent. The collection rate for fiscal year 2024 was 97 percent. The District expects to maintain
8: a 97 percent collection rate.
8: With the 2017 fiscal year, the Houston Independent School District began paying recapture, now known as
8: “contracted instructional services between public schools”. Under the current state finance system, the District
8: sends funds to the state if local revenue exceeds its entitlement. As of June 30, 2024, the District’s “contracted
8: instructional services between public schools” expense was $0.
8: Board of Education
8: November 14, 2024

9: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
9: ACCOUNTING AND BUDGETARY CONTROLS
9: The diverse nature of the District’s operations and the necessity of legal compliance preclude recording the financial
9: transactions of the District in a single fund. The District’s accounting system is organized and operated on a “fund
9: basis.” Each fund is a distinct, self-balancing accounting entity. The various funds are fully described in Note 1 of
9: the “Notes to the Basic Financial Statements” starting on page 39.
9: In developing, evaluating, and improving the District’s accounting system, consideration is given to the adequacy
9: of the internal control structure. Internal controls are designed to provide management with reasonable, but not
9: absolute, assurance that assets are safeguarded against loss from unauthorized use or disposition and those
9: transactions are executed in accordance with management’s authorization and recorded properly to permit the
9: preparation of financial statements in accordance with GAAP. The concept of reasonable assurance recognizes
9: that the cost of a control should not exceed the benefits likely to be derived, and the evaluation of costs and benefits
9: requires estimates and judgments by management.
9: All internal control evaluations occur within the above-mentioned framework. We believe the District’s internal
9: control structure adequately safeguards assets and provides reasonable assurance of the proper recording of
9: financial transactions.
9: As a recipient of federal, state, and local grants, the District is also responsible for an adequate internal control
9: structure that ensures compliance with applicable laws and regulations related to these grants.All funded grants are
9: subject to testing as part of the District’s Single Audit as mandated by the Uniform Guidance. The internal control
9: structure is subject to ongoing reviews by management. As a part of the District’s Single Audit, tests are conducted
9: to determine the adequacy of the internal control structure as related to state and federal award programs as well
9: as compliance with applicable laws and regulations. The SingleAudit reports are included in the compliance section
9: of this report.
9: The District utilizes a line-item budget of proposed expenditures and the means of financing them. The emphasis
9: of the budget process is to identify the activities requiring resources and to rank those activities according to the
9: needs of the entire District. Budgetary control is maintained at the function level by organizational unit through the
9: encumbrance of estimated purchase amounts to coincide with the execution of contracts, approval of personnel
9: transactions, or release of purchase orders to vendors. The Board approves annual budgets for the General Fund,
9: Debt Service Fund, and Nutrition Services Fund. Budgets for Special Revenue funds (other than the Food Service
9: Fund) and Capital Projects Fund are prepared on a project basis, based on grant regulations or applicable bond
9: requirements.
9: MAJOR INITIATIVES
9: In addition to appointing the Board of Managers in June 2023, the Texas Education Commissioner, Mike Morath,
9: also named Mike Miles as the new superintendent of Houston ISD. Mr. Miles has introduced Destination 2035
9: which is designed around Year 2035 competencies and The New Education System (NES) that provides resources
9: and support to dramatically improve outcomes for students. These resources and support include:
9: • Increased salaries, incentive payments, and stipends for teachers
9: • More support for teachers:
9: 1.Discipline handled by administrators
9: 2.Lesson plans and materials provided by curriculum developers (grades 2-10)
9: 3.Differentiated assignments copies made by support personnel
9: 4.Papers graded by support personnel
9: Board of Education
9: November 14, 2024
10: - 5 -
10: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
10: Board of Education
10: November 14, 2024
10: • Innovative staffing model:
10: 1.Differentiated compensation for NES staff based on the hospital model vs.traditional salary
10: schedules for PUA staff
10: 2.Eliminate non-instructional tasks from principals at NES campuses
10: 3.Engage community consultants for non-instructional subjects or electives
10: 4.Expand the reach of the best and brightest teachers
10: 5.Hire additional “Apprentice Teachers”
10: 6.Create the learning coach position
10: As a result of the NES implementation, HISD demonstrated notable academic progress with 80 campuses rising
10: from D or F ratings to A or B ratings and one campus previously rated C also achieving higher performance. Most
10: impressively, three campuses achieved a remarkable leap from F ratings to A ratings.
10: HISD’s 2024 school ratings highlight a positive trend in the district’s overall academic performance. Out of 264
10: campuses scored, 193 scored improvements compared to the previous year, reflecting the District’s commitment to
10: raising academic standards. The number of schools rated D or F was significantly reduced from 121 in 2023 to just
10: 41 in 2024. Additionally, HISD reported a jump in A- and B- rated schools, increasing from 93 last year to 170 this
10: year, indicating a district-wide move toward higher academic achievement.
10: AWARDS AND ACKNOWLEDGMENTS
10: Perhaps no responsibility is as great as that of educating our youth, and all of our efforts lead back to the classroom.
10: Given that the District’s academic advancements are dependent upon its fiscal successes, it is imperative that HISD
10: continue to improve upon its reporting proficiencies. The following text cites awards received by the District for
10: financial reporting excellence and the related acknowledgments in this regard.
10: ASBO Certificate of Excellence
10: The District received the Association of School Business Officials’ (“ASBO”) Certificate of Excellence in Financial
10: Reporting for the fiscal year ended June 30, 2023. The District has received this award for 45 consecutive years.
10: This award certifies that the Annual Comprehensive Financial Report substantially conforms to the principles and
10: standards of financial reporting as recommended and adopted by ASBO.
10: GFOA Certificate of Achievement
10: The Government Finance Officers Association of the United States and Canada (“GFOA”) awarded a Certificate
10: of Achievement for Excellence in Financial Reporting to the District for its Annual Comprehensive Financial Report
10: for the fiscal year ended June 30, 2023. This was the 51st consecutive year that the District has achieved this
10: prestigious award.
10: GTOT Certificate of Distinction
10: The District received the Government Treasurers’Organization of Texas (GTOT) Certificate of Distinction in October
10: 2024. The certificate is good for a two-year period. This award recognized the District’s commitment to maintaining
10: a comprehensive written investment policy that meets the criteria set forth in the GTOT Investment Policy Checklist.
10: Acknowledgments
10: The preparation of this report on a timely basis could not be accomplished without the dedicated services of the
10: entire staff of the District’s Office of Finance. Also, we would like to express our appreciation to other departments,
10: schools, and individuals who assisted in its preparation.

20: Independent Auditor’s Report
20: To the School Board and Management of
20: Houston Independent School District
20: Report on the Audit of the Financial Statements
20: Opinions
20: We have audited the financial statements of the governmental activities, the business-type activities,
20: each major fund, and the aggregate remaining fund information of Houston Independent School District
20: (theDistrict), as of and for the year ended June 30, 2024, and the related notes to the financial statements,
20: which collectively comprise the District’s basic financial statements as listed in the table of contents.
20: In our opinion, the accompanying financial statements present fairly, in all material respects, the
20: respective financial position of the governmental activities, the business-type activities, each major fund,
20: and the aggregate remaining fund information of the District, as of June 30, 2024, and the respective
20: changes in financial position and, where applicable, cash flows thereof for the year then ended in
20: accordance with accounting principles generally accepted in the United States of America.
20: Basis for Opinions
20: We conducted our audit in accordance with auditing standards generally accepted in the
20: United States of America (GAAS) and the standards applicable to financial audits contained in
20: Government Auditing Standards, issued by the Comptroller General of the United States. Our
20: responsibilities under those standards are further described in the Auditor’s Responsibilities for the Audit of
20: the Financial Statements section of our report. We are required to be independent of the District and to
20: meet our other ethical responsibilities, in accordance with the relevant ethical requirements relating to
20: our audit. We believe that the audit evidence we have obtained is sufficient and appropriate to provide
20: a basis for our audit opinions.
20: Responsibilities of Management for the Financial Statements
20: The District’s management is responsible for the preparation and fair presentation of the financial
20: statements in accordance with accounting principles generally accepted in the United States of
20: America, and for the design, implementation, and maintenance of internal control relevant to the
20: preparation and fair presentation of financial statements that are free from material misstatement,
20: whether due to fraud or error.
20: In preparing the financial statements, management is required to evaluate whether there are conditions
20: or events, considered in the aggregate, that raise substantial doubt about the District’s ability to continue
20: as a going concern for twelve months beyond the financial statement date, including any currently
20: known information that may raise substantial doubt shortly thereafter.

21: Auditor’s Responsibilities for the Audit of the Financial Statements
21: Our objectives are to obtain reasonable assurance about whether the financial statements as a whole
21: are free from material misstatement, whether due to fraud or error, and to issue an auditor’s report that
21: includes our opinions. Reasonable assurance is a high level of assurance but is not absolute assurance
21: and therefore is not a guarantee that an audit conducted in accordance with GAAS and Government
21: Auditing Standards will always detect a material misstatement when it exists. The risk of not detecting a
21: material misstatement resulting from fraud is higher than for one resulting from error, as fraud may involve
21: collusion, forgery, intentional omissions, misrepresentations, or the override of internal control.
21: Misstatements are considered material if there is a substantial likelihood that, individually or in the
21: aggregate, they would influence the judgment made by a reasonable user based on the financial
21: statements.
21: In performing an audit in accordance with GAAS and Government Auditing Standards, we:
21:  Exercise professional judgment and maintain professional skepticism throughout the audit.
21:  Identify and assess the risks of material misstatement of the financial statements, whether due to
21: fraud or error, and design and perform audit procedures responsive to those risks. Such procedures
21: include examining, on a test basis, evidence regarding the amounts and disclosures in the
21: financial statements.
21:  Obtain an understanding of internal control relevant to the audit in order to design audit
21: procedures that are appropriate in the circumstances, but not for the purpose of expressing an
21: opinion on the effectiveness of the District’s internal control. Accordingly, no such opinion is
21: expressed.
21:  Evaluate the appropriateness of accounting policies used and the reasonableness of significant
21: accounting estimates made by management, as well as evaluate the overall presentation of the
21: financial statements.
21:  Conclude whether, in our judgment, there are conditions or events, considered in the aggregate,
21: that raise substantial doubt about the District’s ability to continue as a going concern for a
21: reasonable period of time.
21: We are required to communicate with those charged with governance regarding, among other matters,
21: the planned scope and timing of the audit, significant audit findings, and certain internal control–related
21: matters that we identified during the audit.

24: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
24: MANAGEMENT’S DISCUSSION AND ANALYSIS
24: (UNAUDITED)
24: Our discussion and analysis of Houston Independent School District’s (the “District”) financial performance
24: provides an overview of the District’s financial activities for the fiscal year ended June 30, 2024. It should be read
24: in conjunction with the transmittal letter at the front of this report and the basic financial statements which follow
24: this section.
24: The Management’s Discussion and Analysis provides information on both government-wide financial statements
24: and fund financial statements.
24: FINANCIAL HIGHLIGHTS
24: • The District’s total net position as presented on the Government-wide Statement of Net Position is $2.350
24: billion. For the fiscal year ended June 30, 2024, the net position of the District decreased by $21.8 million.
24: • The District’s governmental funds financial statements reported a combined ending fund balance of
24: $1.357 billion. This balance consists of $1.047 billion in the General Fund of which $262.7 million is
24: assigned, $70 million is restricted, $24.5 million is nonspendable, $99.8 million is committed, and $590
24: million is unassigned and available for spending at the District’s discretion. The remaining restricted fund
24: balance of $305.7 million consists of the Special Revenue Fund, the Debt Service Fund, and the Capital
24: Project Fund. The Capital Project Fund’s fund balance also includes $3.99 million in assigned.
24: • For the fiscal year ended June 30, 2024, total revenue and transfers from governmental activities in the
24: Statement of Activities was $3.133 billion. Program revenues accounted for $919 million. General
24: revenues and transfers accounted for $2.214 billion.
24: • The General Fund had $1.983 billion in revenues of which $1.507 billion is property taxes. General Fund
24: expenditures and other financing sources were $2.149 billion and $86.734 million, respectively. This
24: resulted in a decrease to fund balance of $79.87 million.
24: • A portion of the debt obligations of the District are rated “Aaa” by Moody’s Investors Service, Inc.
24: (“Moody’s”) by virtue of a guarantee by the Texas Permanent School Fund Bond Guarantee Program.
24: The outstanding unenhanced tax-supported debt of the District is rated “Aaa” by Moody’s. In addition,
24: lease revenue bonds issued by the District’s Public Facility Corporation received ratings of “Aaa” by
24: Moody’s. Rating information referenced is as of the last rating reviews in March of 2024 by Moody’s.
24: OVERVIEW OF THE FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
24: This discussion and analysis is intended to serve as an introduction to the District’s basic financial statements.
24: The District’s basic financial statements are comprised of three components: 1) government-wide financial
24: statements, 2) fund financial statements, and 3) notes to the basic financial statements. This report also contains
24: required supplementary information and other supplementary information in addition to the basic financial
24: statements themselves.
24: Government-Wide Financial Statements
24: The government-wide financial statements are designed to provide readers a broad overview of the District’s
24: finances in a manner similar to a private-sector business and are comprised of the Statement of Net Position and
24: the Statement of Activities. These statements provide information about the activities of the District as a whole
24: and present both long-term and short-term information about the District’s overall financial status. The District’s
24: basic services are primarily financed by property taxes and include instruction, instructional resources and media
24: services, instructional staff development, instructional leadership, school leadership, guidance, counseling, and
24: evaluation services, social work services, health services, student transportation, food service, co-
24: Management’s Discussion and Analysis (Unaudited)
25: - 18 -
25: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
25: curricular/extracurricular activities, general administration, plant maintenance and operations, security and
25: monitoring services, data processing services, community services, fiscal agent/member districts of shared
25: services, juvenile justice alternative education program, tax reinvestment zone payments, tax appraisal and
25: collection, and interest on long-term debt.
25: The Statement of Net Position presents information on all the District’s assets, deferred outflows, liabilities, and
25: deferred inflows with the difference reported as net position. Over time, increases or decreases in net position
25: may serve as a useful indicator of whether the District’s financial position is improving or deteriorating. The
25: Statement of Net Position includes the District’s non-fiduciary assets and liabilities.
25: The Statement of Activities presents information for all the current fiscal year’s revenues and expenses. This
25: activity is reported as soon as the underlying event giving rise to the activity occurs, regardless of the timing of
25: related cash flows. Therefore, revenues and expenses are reported in this statement for some items that will
25: result in cash flows in future fiscal periods (e.g., uncollected taxes and earned but unused vacation leave).
25: Both of the government-wide financial statements distinguish functions of the District that are principally supported
25: by taxes and intergovernmental revenues (governmental activities) from business-type activities that are intended
25: to recover all or a significant portion of their costs through user fees and charges.
25: Fund Financial Statements
25: The fund financial statements focus on individual parts of the government, reporting the District’s operations in
25: more detail than the government-wide statements. Funds are accounting devices that the District uses to keep
25: track of specific sources of funding and spending for particular purposes. Some funds are required by state law
25: and by bond covenants. The Board of Trustees establishes other funds to control and manage resources and
25: expenditures for particular purposes or to delineate the use of certain taxes and grants.
25: The District has three kinds of funds:
25: Governmental funds are used to account for essentially the same functions reported as governmental activities
25: in the government-wide financial statements. Most of the District’s activities are included in governmental funds
25: which focus on (1) how cash and other financial assets can readily be converted to cash flow in and out, and (2)
25: the balances left at year end that are available. However, unlike the government-wide financial statements,
25: governmental fund financial statements provide a detailed short-term view that helps determine whether there are
25: more or fewer financial resources that can be spent in the near future to finance the District’s programs. Because
25: this information does not encompass the additional long-term focus of the government-wide statements, we
25: provide additional information on the subsequent page that explains the relationship (or differences) between
25: them.
25: The District maintains four governmental funds. Information is presented separately in the governmental fund
25: balance sheet and in the governmental fund statement of revenues, expenditures, and changes in fund balances
25: for the General Fund, the Special Revenue Fund, the Debt Service Fund, and the Capital Project Fund, all of
25: which are considered to be major funds.
25: Proprietary funds are used to account for operations that are financed similarly to those found in the private
25: sector. These funds provide both long and short-term financial information. The District maintains two types of
25: proprietary funds. Enterprise funds are used to report the same type of information as reported in the government-
25: wide financial statements. The District uses enterprise funds to account for the employee cafeteria (MarketPlace)
25: and Medicaid programs. The District uses internal service funds to report activities that provide services for the
25: District’s other programs and activities, i.e., health insurance, workers’ compensation, print shop, and other
25: internal services. Because these services predominately benefit governmental rather than business-type
25: functions, they have been included within governmental activities within the government-wide financial
25: statements.
25: Fiduciary funds are used to account for assets held by the District in a trustee capacity or as a custodian for
25: individuals, private organizations and/or other funds. The District uses fiduciary funds to account for scholarship
25: funds and student activity funds. No fiduciary funds are used as clearing accounts to distribute financial resources
25: to other funds. The District is responsible for ensuring that the assets reported in these funds are used for their
25: intended purposes. All the District’s fiduciary activities are reported in a separate statement of fiduciary net
26: - 19 -

26: position and a statement of changes in fiduciary net position. We exclude these activities from the District’s
26: government-wide financial statements because the District cannot use these assets to finance its operations.
26: Notes to the Basic Financial Statements
26: The notes to the basic financial statements provide additional information that is essential to a complete
26: understanding of the data provided in the government-wide and fund financial statements.
26: Other Information
26: In addition to the basic financial statements and accompanying notes, this report also presents certain required
26: supplementary information that further explains and supports the information in the financial statements.
26: Immediately following the required supplementary information, combining statements are included for the
26: nonmajor enterprise funds and the internal service funds.

27: GOVERNMENT-WIDE FINANCIAL ANALYSIS
27: As noted earlier, net position may serve as a useful indicator of the District’s financial position. The District’s
27: combined net position decreased $21.8 million. The District’s net investment in capital assets is $2.306 billion
27: and includes investments in capital assets (e.g. land, buildings and improvements, furniture, equipment, right-to-
27: use assets, and construction in progress) less any related debt used to acquire those assets that is still
27: outstanding.
27: The District uses these capital assets to provide services to students; consequently, these assets are not available
27: for future spending. Although the District’s investment in its capital assets is reported net of related debt, it should
27: be noted that the resources needed to repay this debt must be provided from other sources, since the capital
27: assets themselves cannot be used to liquidate these liabilities.
27: The District’s restricted net position of $217 million represents resources that are subject to external restrictions
27: on how they may be used. The remaining $(172.5) million is unrestricted net position.

28: The District’s total revenues were $3.126 billion. A significant portion, approximately 60 percent, of the District’s
28: revenue is from property taxes. Operating grants and contributions provided 29 percent while another 7 percent
28: is provided by state aid, 3 percent from interest income, and 1 precent from other miscellaneous revenue.
28: Total revenues decreased by $253 million from the prior year due primarily to decreases in property tax
28: collections.
28: Governmental Activities
28: The net position of the District’s governmental activities decreased by a net $7.281 million from current operations
28: due primarily to a decrease in property tax collections and an increase in instruction expenditures. Funding for
28: governmental activities is by specific program revenue or through general revenues such as property taxes and
28: investment earnings. The following is a summary of the governmental activities:
28: • The cost of all governmental activities this year was $3.14 billion.
28: • Some of the governmental activities cost was funded by program revenues directly attributable to specific
28: activities. These program revenues amounted to $919 million.
28: • The remaining cost of governmental activities not directly funded by program revenues was $2.221 billion
28: including $1.867 billion funded by property taxes and $201 million funded by state aid which is not
28: restricted to specific programs.
28: Business-type Activities
28: The net position of the District’s business-type activities decreased by $14.482 million due primarily to a transfer
28: of $14 million to the general fund. The following is a summary of the business-type activities:
28: • The cost of all business-type activities this year was $7.246 million.
28: • The specific program revenue directly attributable to specific activities was $5.665 million.
28: The District’s total revenues were $3.126 billion. A significant portion, approximately 60 percent, of the District’s
28: revenue is from property taxes. Operating grants and contributions provided 29 percent while another 7 percent
28: is provided by state aid, 3 percent from interest income, and 1 precent from other miscellaneous revenue.
28: Total revenues decreased by $253 million from the prior year due primarily to decreases in property tax
28: collections.
28: Governmental Activities
28: The net position of the District’s governmental activities decreased by a net $7.281 million from current operations
28: due primarily to a decrease in property tax collections and an increase in instruction expenditures. Funding for
28: governmental activities is by specific program revenue or through general revenues such as property taxes and
28: investment earnings. The following is a summary of the governmental activities:
28: • The cost of all governmental activities this year was $3.14 billion.
28: • Some of the governmental activities cost was funded by program revenues directly attributable to specific
28: activities. These program revenues amounted to $919 million.
28: • The remaining cost of governmental activities not directly funded by program revenues was $2.221 billion
28: including $1.867 billion funded by property taxes and $201 million funded by state aid which is not
28: restricted to specific programs.

28: The net position of the District’s business-type activities decreased by $14.482 million due primarily to a transfer

28: • The cost of all business-type activities this year was $7.246 million.
28: • The specific program revenue directly attributable to specific activities was $5.665 million.
28: The District’s total revenues were $3.126 billion. A significant portion, approximately 60 percent, of the District’s
28: revenue is from property taxes. Operating grants and contributions provided 29 percent while another 7 percent
28: is provided by state aid, 3 percent from interest income, and 1 precent from other miscellaneous revenue.
28: Total revenues decreased by $253 million from the prior year due primarily to decreases in property tax
28: collections.
28: Governmental Activities
28: The net position of the District’s governmental activities decreased by a net $7.281 million from current operations
28: due primarily to a decrease in property tax collections and an increase in instruction expenditures. Funding for
28: governmental activities is by specific program revenue or through general revenues such as property taxes and
28: investment earnings. The following is a summary of the governmental activities:
28: • The cost of all governmental activities this year was $3.14 billion.
28: • Some of the governmental activities cost was funded by program revenues directly attributable to specific
28: activities. These program revenues amounted to $919 million.
28: • The remaining cost of governmental activities not directly funded by program revenues was $2.221 billion
28: including $1.867 billion funded by property taxes and $201 million funded by state aid which is not
28: restricted to specific programs.
28: Business-type Activities
28: The net position of the District’s business-type activities decreased by $14.482 million due primarily to a transfer
28: of $14 million to the general fund. The following is a summary of the business-type activities:
28: • The cost of all business-type activities this year was $7.246 million.
28: • The specific program revenue directly attributable to specific activities was $5.665 million.

31: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
31: FINANCIAL ANALYSIS OF THE DISTRICT’S FUNDS
31: Governmental Funds For the fiscal year ended June 30, 2024, the District’s governmental funds reported ending
31: fund balances of $1.357 billion. Of this amount, $590 million is unassigned and available for use in activities at
31: the District’s discretion. The remainder of the fund balance is designated as nonspendable, restricted, committed,
31: or assigned, which indicates that it is not available for new spending because it has already been obligated for
31: grants, debt service, bond projects and other purposes of the District.
31: The General Fund is the primary operating fund of the District. The fund balance of the General Fund decreased
31: $79.872 million during the fiscal year. At the end of the fiscal year, the unassigned fund balance of $590 million
31: represented 28 percent of the total General Fund expenditures.
31: The Debt Service Fund has a total fund balance of $123.8 million all of which is restricted for the payment of debt
31: service. The fund balance during the fiscal year decreased by $2.8 million. The District makes semi-annual debt
31: service payments in December/June, January/July, February/August and March/September of each year. Debt
31: service payments for the fiscal year ended June 30, 2024 included all scheduled payments including payments
31: due within 30 days of year end.
31: The Special Revenue Fund accounts for all grants received by the District from local, state, and federal sources.
31: The balance increased by $3.1 million to $114 million due to an increase in federal funds.
31: The Capital Project Fund has a total fund balance of $71.6 million. The restricted fund balance at the end of the
31: fiscal year totaled $67.6 million and represents bond funds for facility construction projects. The assigned fund
31: balance totaled $3.9 million and represents funds for the Pay-As-You-Go Capital Program. The fund balance
31: decreased by $141.7 million due to expenditures for bond construction projects and transfer of $70 million of TIRZ
31: funds to the General Fund.
31: Proprietary Funds The District maintains both enterprise funds and internal service funds. Information is
31: presented separately in the proprietary funds statement of net position and in the proprietary funds statement of
31: revenues, expenses, and changes in net position. See pages 34 and 35.

32: As of June 30, 2024, the District had fully funded construction commitments of $41.3 million, $33.7 million of
32: which has been spent to date and $7.6 million in remaining commitment. More detailed information about the
32: District’s capital assets is presented in Note 6 of the “Notes to the Basic Financial Statements.” See pages 58 –

32: Long Term Debt (See Note 8)
32: As of June 30, 2024, the District had bonded debt outstanding of $1.670 billion. The ratio of net general bonded
32: debt to assessed valuation and the amount of bonded debt per student are useful indicators of the District's
32: debt position. This data reflects a decrease in the ratio of net bonded debt to assessed value to 0.74 percent,
32: as compared to 0.90 percent last year.
32: The District debt totals included $190.435 million in Variable Rate Bonds outstanding at fiscal year-end.
32: Outstanding variable rate debt decreased by $45.675 million compared to prior year ending balances as a result
32: of redemptions utilizing available debt service funds and sinking fund redemptions. Variable rate debt as a
32: percentage of net bonded debt outstanding as of June 30, 2024, is 10.30 percent. In addition, on May 31, 2024,
32: the District redeemed $68.72 million par of fixed rate bonds utilizing available debt service funds
32: The District did not issue any maintenance tax notes during the year ended June 30, 2024. The remaining
32: balance of maintenance tax notes issued previously is $143.21 million as of June 30, 2024.
32: The District did not issue any contractual obligations during the year ended June 30, 2024.
32: Interest earnings on proceeds from debt issued since May 15, 1989, are subject to arbitrage regulations
32: contained in the Federal Tax Reform Act of 1986. The District did not identify arbitrage liability as of June 30,
32: 2024.
33: - 26 -
33: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
33: General Fund Budgetary Highlights
33: Over the course of the year, the District revised its budget several times. Significant budget adjustments include:
33: • Decrease in property tax revenues of $342.0 million due to the increase in exemptions and refunds
33: • Increase in foundation state revenues of $71.4 million
33: • Increase in expenditure budget due to carryover encumbrances of 117.7 million
33: • Increase due to New Education System (NES) implementation of $69.2 million
33: • Decrease of $18.2 million in performance contract schools reclass to ESSER
33: • Decrease in recapture of $326.5 million
33: • Increase of $27.9 million for facilities maintenance and transportation costs
33: • Increase in insurance proceeds from previous years’ weather events in the amount of $16 million
33: • Increase in transfers from other funds in the amount of $60 million
33: Total actual expenditures were $78.0 million less than the final budget. Following were the key variances:
33: • $61.0 million under budget in school and department non-salary spending
33: • $17.0 million in FY 2024 purchase orders which were carried forward and will be paid in FY 2025
33: The 2023-2024 final expenditure budget decreased by $131.9 million from the prior year budget.
33: The final budgeted amount for revenues was $1.942 billion. Actual revenues were greater than the budget by
33: $40.1 million primarily due to:
33: • $23.8 million under budget in property tax collections
33: • $12.2 million increase in interest earnings
33: • $25.3 million increase in the Foundation School Program
33: • $12.9 million increase in FEMA receipts related to the COVID pandemic
33: Budget amendments are presented to the Board of Education for consideration and approval throughout the year.
33: ECONOMIC FACTORS AND NEXT YEAR’S BUDGETS AND RATES
33: The District’s General Fund is funded primarily between local property taxes and state revenue. Through the
33: state’s funding formula, a school district receives a basic allotment for each student with weights added to that
33: allotment if the student receives additional services such as special education, at-risk (state compensatory
33: education) bilingual, dyslexia, early education, college, career or military readiness outcomes, career and
33: technology and transportation, which determines the district’s Tier I allotment.
33: The 2024-2025 General Fund budget, adopted in June 2024, included projected decreases of $63.6 million in
33: revenues and $127 million in expenditures. The state revenue projections are based on an estimated average
33: daily attendance (ADA) of 160,734 or 234,346 based on a weighted average daily attendance (WADA).
33: In FY2024-2025, HISD’s total expenditures are estimated at $2.116 billion.
33: Actions of the 88th Legislature in 2023:
33: 1. No changes to the basic allotment at $6,160 for 2023-2024 and 2024-2025. This base amount is
33: generated by every student in HISD.
33: 2. Increased school safety funding from $9.72 per ADA to $10 per ADA. Added additional safety funding of
33: $15,000 per campus
33: 3. Increased the state homestead exemption from $40,000 to $100,000 which was subsequently approved
33: by voters in November 2023.
34: - 27 -
34: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
34: 4. Reduced the Maximum Compression Rate (MCR) by an additional $0.1070 for tax year 2023, subject to
34: the 90% equity band.
34: 5. Included Maintenance and Operations and Interest Sinking Hold Harmless (covers debt approved by
34: voters as of September 1, 2023.)
34: Tax Rate
34: At budget adoption in June, it was projected that the total district tax rate per $100 of property value would be
34: $0.8683. This assumed the Maintenance and Operations (M&O rate) of $0.7016 consisting of a maximum
34: compressed rate of $0.6516 plus five Tier II “Golden Pennies “as well as an interest and sinking (I&S) rate of
34: $0.1667. These rates were approved by the Board of Managers in August.
34: CONTACTING THE DISTRICT’S FINANCIAL MANAGEMENT
34: This financial report is designed to provide our citizens, taxpayers, customers, investors, and creditors with a
34: general overview of the District’s finances and to demonstrate the District’s accountability for the money it
34: receives. If you have questions about this report or need additional financial information, contact the Chief
34: Finance and Operations Officer at 4400 West 18th Street, Houston, Texas 77092, or call (713) 556-5500.

40: Amounts reported for governmental activities in the statement of activities are different because:
40: Governmental funds report capital outlays as expenditures. However, in the statement of activities,
40: the cost of those assets is allocated over their estimated useful lives as depreciation and amortization expense.
40: This is the amount by which capital assets changed in the current period.
40: Facilities acquisition and construction and other acquisitions 106,473,424
40: Disposals (72,324,711)

40: increases long-term liabilities in the statement of net position.
40: Issuance of leases and subscriptions (2,292,919) (2,292,919)
40: Repayment of principal is an expenditure in the governmental funds, but the repayment reduces
40: long-term liabilities in the statement of net position. Lease and subscription terminations reduce
40: long-term liabilities in statement of net position, but are not current financial resources in the
40: governmental funds.

40: Bonds payable 302,755,000

40: governmental funds because interest is recognized as an expenditure in the funds when it is due,
40: and thus, requires the use of current financial resources. In the statement of activities, however
40: interest expense is recognized as the interest accrues, regardless of when it is due. The changes
40: reported in the statement of activities consists of the following:

46: Notes to the Basic Financial Statements
46: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
46: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
46: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2024
46: NOTE 1: SUMMARY OF SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES
46: REPORTING ENTITY
46: The Houston Independent School District (“District”) is an independent school district created and
46: incorporated through legislation enacted by the Thirty-Eighth Texas State Legislature in 1923. The
46: District is the largest school district in Texas and is governed by the Board of Managers, appointed
46: by the Texas Commissioner of Education in June 2023.
46: In accordance with GovernmentalAccounting Standards, a financial reporting entity consists of the
46: primary government and its component units. Component units are legally separate organizations
46: for which the elected officials of the District are financially accountable, or for which the relationship
46: to the District is such that exclusion would cause the District’s financial statements to be misleading
46: or incomplete. The District’s blended component unit, although a legally separate entity, is in
46: substance a part of the District’s operations, and so data from this entity is combined with data of
46: the primary government.
46: The criteria used to determine whether an organization is a component unit of the District includes:
46: financial accountability of the District for the component unit, whether the District appoints a voting
46: majority of the entity’s board, the ability to impose the District’s will on the component unit, fiscal
46: dependency criterion, if it is a financial benefit to or burden to the District, and whether services are
46: provided entirely or almost entirely to the District.
46: For financial reporting purposes, the Houston Independent School District GEAR UP Trust (“GEAR
46: UP Trust”) is included in the fiduciary activities of the District because of the financial accountability
46: of the District, the appointment of a voting majority, and the fiscal dependency criteria whereby the
46: GEAR UP Trust serves the District’s students exclusively. The GEAR UP Trust was incorporated
46: to ensure that the scholarship funds are properly spent and to guarantee a college scholarship to
46: the eligible students at participating schools of the GEAR UP program. The GEAR UP financial
46: information is blended with that of the District in the Private-Purpose Trust Funds column in the
46: Statement of Fiduciary Net Position. Separate financial statements for GEAR UP Trust are not
46: issued.
46: IMPLEMENTATION OF NEWACCOUNTING PRONOUNCEMENTS
46: GASB Statement No. 99, Omnibus 2022 (GASB 99), enhances comparability in accounting and
46: financial reporting and improves consistency of authoritative literature by addressing 1) practice
46: issues that have been identified during implementation and application of certain GASB statements
46: and 2) accounting and financial reporting for financial guarantees. The requirements related to
46: extension of the use of LIBOR, accounting for SNAP distributions, disclosures of nonmonetary
46: transactions, pledges of future revenues by pledging governments, clarification of certain provisions
46: in Statement 34, as amended, and terminology updates related to Statement 53 and Statement 63
46: are effective upon issuance. The requirements related to leases were implemented in the District’s
46: fiscal year 2022 financial statements in conjunction with GASB 87. The requirements related to
46: PPPs and SBITAs are effective for fiscal years beginning after June 15, 2022, and all reporting
46: periods thereafter. These requirements for GASB 99 were implemented in the District’s fiscal year
46: 2023 financial statements in conjunction with GASB 94 and GASB 96. The requirements related

47: GOVERNMENT-WIDEAND FUND FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
47: The Statement of Net Position and the Statement of Activities of the government-wide financial
47: statements report information on all the nonfiduciary activities of the District and its blended
47: component unit. The District eliminates from the Statement of Net Position and the Statement of
47: Activities most interfund receivables, payables, and transfers between funds. Interfund services
47: provided and used are eliminated in the process of consolidation. Governmental activities, which
47: normally are supported by taxes and intergovernmental revenues, are reported separately from
47: business-type activities, which rely to a significant extent on fees and charges for support.
47: The Statement of Activities demonstrates the degree to which the direct expenses of a given
47: function are offset by program revenues. Program revenues include (1) charges to customers
47: or applicants for goods or services provided, and (2) grants and contributions that are restricted
47: to meeting operational requirements of a particular function. Program revenues included in the
47: Statement of Activities reduce the cost of the function to be financed from general revenues. Taxes
47: and other items not restricted to specific programs are properly excluded from program revenues
47: and reported instead as general revenues.

48: The District reports all direct expenses by function in the Statement of Activities. Direct
48: expenses are those clearly identifiable with a function. Depreciation and amortization expense
48: is specifically identified by function and is included in the direct expense to each function.
48: Separate financial statements are provided for governmental funds, proprietary funds, and fiduciary
48: funds. In accordance with the provisions of GASB Statement No. 34, the fiduciary funds are

48: MEASUREMENT FOCUS, BASIS OF ACCOUNTING AND FINANCIAL STATEMENT
48: PRESENTATION
48: Government-wide Financial Statements - The government-wide financial statements are reported
48: using the economic resources measurement focus and the accrual basis of accounting. Revenues,
48: expenses, gains, losses, assets, deferred outflows of resources, liabilities, and deferred inflows
48: of resources resulting from exchange and exchange-like transactions are recognized when the
48: exchange takes place, regardless of the timing of the related cash flows. Revenues, expenses,
48: gains, losses, assets, deferred outflows of resources, liabilities, and deferred inflows of resources
48: resulting from nonexchange transactions are recognized in accordance with GASB Statement No.
48: 33, as amended. Property taxes are recognized as revenues in the year for which they are levied.
48: Grants, and similar items, are recognized as revenue as soon as all eligibility requirements have
48: been met and funds are available to meet current expenditures.
48: Fund Financial Statements - Governmental fund financial statements are reported using the current
48: financial resources measurement focus and the modified accrual basis of accounting. Revenues
48: are recognized as soon as they are both measurable and available. State aid and miscellaneous
48: revenues, other than property taxes and state and federal grants, are considered to be available
48: if collected within the current period or within 90 days of the fiscal year end to pay liabilities of the
48: current period. Property taxes are considered to be available if collected within 60 days of the fiscal
48: year end. State and federal grants are considered to be available if collected within 120 days of
48: the fiscal year end. Expenditures are generally recognized under the modified accrual basis of
48: accounting in the accounting period in which the fund liability is incurred, if measurable. However,
48: debt service expenditures are recognized in the current fiscal period for debt service principal and
48: interest payments due within 30 days in the subsequent fiscal period as these funds have been set
48: aside for that purpose. Compensated absences and claims and judgments are recorded only when
48: payment is due. Both Proprietary fund statements and Fiduciary fund statements are reported
48: using the economic resources measurement focus and the accrual basis of accounting.
48: FUNDS
48: The District’s accounting system is organized and operated with the use of funds, each of which
48: is a separate accounting entity with a self-balancing set of accounts. The District’s resources are
48: allocated to and accounted for in individual funds based on the intended purposes for spending
48: and the means by which spending activities are controlled. The following fund types are included
48: in the financial statements:
48: Governmental Funds
48: Governmental Funds are those through which most governmental functions of the District are
48: financed. The acquisition, use, and balances of the District’s expendable financial resources, and
48: the related liabilities are accounted for through governmental funds. The following are the District’s
48: major governmental funds:
48: • General Fund - The General Fund is the general operating fund of the District and accounts
48: for all revenues and expenditures of the District not encompassed within other funds.
48: • Special Revenue Fund - The Special Revenue Fund is used to account for and report the
48: proceeds of specific revenue sources that are restricted or committed to expenditures
49: - 42 -
49: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
49: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
49: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
49: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2024
49: for specified purposes other than debt service or capital projects. The District’s Special
49: Revenue Fund includes the National School Breakfast and Lunch Program, United States
49: Department of Education grants, Campus Activity Funds, and all other federal, state and
49: locally funded grants and programs. These grants are awarded to the District for the purpose
49: of accomplishing specific educational tasks as defined in the grant awards. The schedules of
49: federalandstateawardsareincludedinthecompliancesectionoftheAnnualComprehensive
49: Financial Report.
49: • Debt Service Fund - The Debt Service Fund is used to account for the accumulation of
49: resources for, and the retirement of, general long-term debt and related costs.
49: • Capital Project Fund - The Capital Project Fund is used to account for financial resources to
49: be used for the acquisition, renovation, or construction of capital facilities.
49: Proprietary Funds
49: Proprietary Funds are used to account for operations that are financed in a manner similar to those
49: found in the private sector, where the determination of net income is appropriate for sound financial
49: administration.
49: • Enterprise Funds - The Enterprise Funds are used to account for operations that are financed
49: and operated similar to a private enterprise where the District’s intent is to provide services
49: financed primarily through user charges. The District’s enterprise funds include Medicaid and
49: the Marketplace activities.
49: • InternalServiceFunds-TheInternalServiceFundsareusedtoaccountforoperationsrelated
49: to the Print Shop, Alternative Certification Program, Athletics, University Interscholastic
49: League (“UIL”), Shared Services, and risk financing activities of the District.
49: Proprietary funds distinguish operating revenues and expenses from nonoperating items.
49: Operating revenues and expenses generally result from providing services and producing and
49: delivering goods in connection with a proprietary fund’s principal ongoing operations. The principal
49: operating revenues of the District’s proprietary funds are charges to customers and other funds
49: for sales and services. Operating expenses for proprietary funds include the cost of sales and
49: services, administrative expenses, and depreciation on capital assets. All revenues and expenses
49: not meeting this definition are reported as nonoperating revenues and expenses.
49: Fiduciary Funds
49: Fiduciary Funds are used to account for assets held by the District in a trustee capacity or as a
49: custodian for individuals, private organizations and/or other funds.
49: • Private-Purpose Trust Funds - Private-purpose trust funds are used to account for resources
49: legally held in trust under which principal and income benefit individuals, private organizations
49: or other governments. The District’s private-purpose trust funds represent scholarship funds
49: being held in trust for students.
49: • Custodial Funds - Custodial funds are used to account for resources that the District has
49: custody of, but is not the district’s own-source revenue, and does not control the use of
49: revenues. The District’s custodial funds represent school class and club activity funds.
49: Management’s Use of Estimates
49: The preparation of financial statements in conformity with generally accepted accounting principles
49: requires management to make estimates and assumptions that affect certain reported amounts
49: and disclosures. Actual results could differ from those estimates.

50: Change in Accounting Estimate
50: In October 2023, the Texas Department of Health and Human Services Commission updated their
50: policy in relation to reimbursement for Medicaid services provided by local education agencies
50: through the School Health and Related Services program, resulting in a decrease in the expected
50: funding from the 2022 federal fiscal year claim submitted in March 2023. This funding policy
50: change resulted in an increase to the allowance for doubtful accounts. The effect of these changes
50: on revenue and changes in net position was a decrease of approximately $14 million for the year
50: ended June 30, 2024.
50: Stewardship, Compliance, and Accountability
50: The District is required by law to prepare and file a budget with the Texas EducationAgency (TEA).
50: Activities of the General Fund, Nutrition Services Fund, and Debt Service Fund are included in the
50: District’s formally adopted budget. Budgets for the Special Revenue Fund (other than Nutrition
50: Services) and Capital Project Fund are approved on a project basis. The District is required to
50: present the adopted and final amended budgeted revenues and expenditures and actual revenues
50: and expenditures for the General Fund, Nutrition Services Fund, and Debt Service Fund. These
50: budgets are in the financial section of the Annual Comprehensive Financial Report.
50: Pensions
50: The fiduciary net position of the Teacher Retirement System of Texas (TRS) has been determined
50: using the flow of economic resources measurement focus and full accrual basis of accounting.
50: This includes for purposes of measuring the net pension liability, deferred outflows of resources
50: and deferred inflows of resources related to pensions, pension expense, and information about
50: assets, liabilities, and additions to/deductions from TRS’s fiduciary net position. Benefit payments
50: (including refunds of employee contributions) are recognized when due and payable in accordance
50: with the benefit terms. Investments are reported at fair value.
50: Other Post-Employment Benefits (OPEB)
50: The fiduciary net position of the Teacher Retirement System of Texas (TRS) Care Plan has been
50: determined using the flow of economic resources measurement focus and full accrual basis of
50: accounting. This includes for purposes of measuring the net OPEB liability, deferred outflows of
50: resources and deferred inflows of resources related to other post-employment benefits, OPEB
50: expense, and information about assets, liabilities, and additions to/deductions from TRS Care’s
50: fiduciary net position. Benefit payments are recognized when due and payable in accordance with
50: the benefit terms. There are no investments as this is a pay-as-you-go plan and all cash is held in
50: a cash account.
50: ASSETS, LIABILITIES, AND DEFERRED OUTLFOWS AND INFLOWS OF RESOURCES
50: Cash and Cash Equivalents
50: The District requires all deposits to be fully collateralized with depository insurance; obligations
50: of the United States of America or its agencies and instrumentalities (excluding those mortgage
50: backed securities prohibited by the Public Funds Investment Act). At all times, such securities are
50: to have a fair value sufficient to fully collateralize deposit balances as adjusted by the amount of
50: applicable depository insurance pursuant to the Collateral for Public FundsAct, Texas Government
50: Code Section 2257.022.
50: The District considers cash and cash equivalents to be cash on hand and demand deposits. All
50: other monetary assets are treated as investments including certificates of deposit, investment pools,
50: money market instruments, and other securities defined under the Public Funds Investment Act.

51: property tax receivable allowance is 52.00 percent of outstanding property taxes at June 30, 2024.
51: Inventories
51: Under the moving average inventory method, the average cost of each inventory item in stock is
51: recalculated after every inventory purchase. This method tends to yield inventory valuations and
51: cost of goods sold that are in-between those derived under the first in, first out (FIFO) method
51: and the last in, last out (LIFO) method. These materials and supplies are subsequently charged
51: to expenditures when consumed. Inventories include instructional materials, plant maintenance
51: operatingsupplies,aswellasnutritionservicesfoodandsupplies.Revenuefordonatedcommodities
51: is recognized when the commodities are received. Non-food inventories are offset at fiscal year-
51: end by non-spendable fund balance in the governmental fund financial statements.
51: OtherAssets
51: Other assets include accrued interest, prepaid insurance, and prepaid travel. Prepaids are charged
51: to expenditures when consumed and are offset at year end by non-spendable fund balance in the
51: governmental fund financial statements.
51: Encumbrances

51: the expenditure of funds are recorded in the accounting system in order to reserve the portion of
51: the applicable appropriation, is employed in the Governmental Fund Types on the governmental
51: fund financial statements. Encumbrances outstanding at year end are reported as restricted or
51: assigned, as appropriate.

52: cost if purchased or constructed. The capitalization threshold is a unit cost of $5,000. Donated

53: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
53: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
53: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
53: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2024
53: Leases
53: The District is a lessee for noncancellable leases of property and equipment.The District recognizes
53: a lease liability, reported with long-term debt, and a right-to-use lease asset (lease asset), reported
53: with other capital assets, in the government-wide and proprietary fund financial statements.
53: The District recognizes lease liabilities with an initial, individual value of $5,000 or more.
53: At the commencement of a lease, the District initially measures the lease liability at the present
53: value of payments expected to be made during the lease term. Subsequently, the lease liability is
53: reduced by the principal portion of lease payments made. The lease asset is initially measured as
53: the initial amount of the lease liability, adjusted for lease payments made at or before the lease
53: commencement date, plus certain initial direct costs. Subsequently, the lease asset is amortized on
53: a straight-line basis over the shorter of the lease term or its useful life.
53: Key estimates and judgments related to leases include how the District determines (1) the discount
53: rate it uses to discount the expected lease payments to present value, (2) lease term, and (3) lease
53: payments.
53: The District uses the interest rate charged by the lessor as the discount rate. When the interest
53: rate charged by the lessor is not provided, the District generally uses its estimated incremental
53: borrowing rate as the discount rate for leases.
53: The lease term includes the noncancellable period of the lease.
53: Lease payments included in the measurement of the lease liability are composed of fixed payments,
53: variable payments fixed in substance or that depend on an index or a rate, purchase option price
53: that the District is reasonably certain to exercise, lease incentives receivable from the lessor, and
53: any other payments that are reasonably certain of being required based on an assessment of all
53: relevant factors.
53: The District monitors changes in circumstances that would require a remeasurement of its leases
53: and will remeasure the lease asset and liability if certain changes occur that are expected to
53: significantly affect the amount of the lease liability.
53: Subscription-Based Information Technology Agreements (SBITAs)
53: The District has noncancellable contracts with SBITA vendors for the right to use information
53: technology (IT) software, alone or in combination with tangible capital assets (the underlying IT
53: assets). The District recognizes a subscription liability, reported with long-term debt, and a right-to-
53: use subscription asset (an intangible asset), reported with other capital assets, in the government-
53: wide and proprietary fund financial statements. The District recognizes subscription liabilities with
53: an initial, individual value of $5,000 or more.
53: At the commencement of an SBITA, the District initially measures the subscription liability at the
53: present value of payments expected to be made during the subscription term. Subsequently, the
53: subscription liability is reduced by the principal portion of SBITA payments made. The subscription
53: asset is initially measured as the initial amount of the subscription liability, adjusted for SBITA
53: payments made at or before the SBITA commencement date, plus certain initial implementation
53: costs. Subsequently, the subscription asset is amortized on a straight-line basis over the shorter of
53: the subscription term or the useful life of the underlying IT assets.
53: Key estimates and judgments related to SBITAs include how the District determines (1) the discount
53: rate it uses to discount the expected subscription payments to present value, (2) subscription term,
53: and (3) subscription payments.
53: The District uses the interest rate charged by the SBITA vendor as the discount rate. When the
53: interest rate charged by the SBITA vendor is not provided, the District generally uses its estimated
53: incremental borrowing rate as the discount rate for SBITAs.

54: The subscription term includes the noncancellable period of the SBITA.
54: Subscription payments included in the measurement of the subscription liability are composed

54: termination penalties if the District is reasonably certain to exercise such options, subscription
54: contract incentives receivable from the SBITAvendor, and any other payments that are reasonably
