54: and will remeasure the subscription asset and liability if certain changes occur that are expected to
54: significantly affect the amount of the subscription liability.
54: Interfund Transactions
54: Activities between funds that are representative of lending/borrowing arrangements outstanding at
54: the end of the fiscal year are referred to as “due to/from other funds.” All other balances between
54: funds are reported as transfers. Any residual balances outstanding between the governmental
54: activities and business-type activities are reported in the government-wide statements as “internal
54: balances.”
54: A description of the basic types of interfund transactions and the related accounting policies are as
54: follows:
54: • Charges for services are reported as revenues for the performing fund and expenditures/
54: expenses of the requesting fund.
54: • Transactions to reimburse a fund for expenditures made by it for the benefit of another fund
54: are recorded as expenditures or expenses in the reimbursing fund and as a reduction of
54: expenditures or expenses in the fund that is reimbursed.
54: • Allotherinterfundtransfers,suchaslegallyauthorizedtransfersfromafundreceivingrevenue
54: to the fund through which the resources are to be expended, are transfers. Transfers are
54: classified as other financing sources or uses in the Statement of Revenues, Expenditures,
54: and Changes in Fund Balances.
54: For reporting at the government-wide financial statement level, the District eliminates direct interfund
54: charges for services and the balances created within the same activity categories (i.e. governmental
54: vs. business-type). This process ensures neither governmental nor business-type activities report
54: direct internal revenue/expenditures. Interfund activity and balances resulting from transactions with
54: thefiduciaryfundsarenoteliminated.Instead,thefiduciaryinterfundactivityandbalancesaretreated
54: as transactions with an external party. The Internal Service Funds are essentially repositories for
54: income, expenses, assets and liabilities of the District’s health insurance, worker’s compensation,
54: print shop, alternative certification, athletics, shared services, and UIL activities.
54: Compensated Absences
54: The District’s contract employees earn one day of personal leave per month for all months under
54: contract, which may either be taken or accumulated with no limitation. Accumulated sick leave
54: balances are paid upon termination from the District to employees meeting certain criteria and hired
54: before October 10, 1972. In addition, the District may “buy-back” accrued but unused state leave in
54: accordance with the “Attendance Incentive Plan.”
54: Twelve-month employees earn vacation ranging from two to four weeks per year based on length
54: of service. Beginning with FY 2021, vacation days may be carried over from one contract year to
54: another with a maximum limit of 50 days compared to previous years when only 30 days could
54: be carried over. For twelve-month employees, the contract year runs from September 1st through
54: August 31st.
55: - 48 -
55: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
55: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
55: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
55: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2024
55: The District accrues vacation, state personal leave, and eligible sick leave in the government-wide
55: and proprietary fund financial statements. A liability and related expenditure for these amounts is
55: reported in the governmental funds only if they have matured as a result of employee retirements
55: or resignations and are due.
55: Long-term Liabilities
55: In the government-wide financial statements, long-term debt and other long-term obligations are
55: reported as liabilities in the Statement of Net Position. Bond premiums and discounts are amortized
55: over the life of the bonds using the effective interest method. Gains and losses on refunds are
55: amortized over the life of the bonds using the straight-line method. Bond issuance costs are
55: expensed as incurred.
55: In the fund financial statements, governmental funds recognize bond premiums and discounts,
55: as well as bond issuance costs, during the current period. The face amount of the debt issued
55: is reported as other financing sources. Premiums received on debt issuances are reported as
55: other financing sources while discounts on debt issuances are reported as other financing uses.
55: Issuance costs, whether or not withheld from the actual debt proceeds received, are reported as
55: debt service expenditures.
55: Deferred Outflows and Inflows of Resources
55: In addition to assets, the Statement of Net Position includes a separate section for deferred outflows
55: of resources which represents a consumption of net assets that applies to a future period(s) and
55: therefore, will not be recognized as an outflow of resources (expenses/expenditures) until then.
55: Included in this category are deferred losses on refunding,TRS pension costs, andTRS OPEB costs.
55: In addition to liabilities, the Statement of Net Position includes a separate section for deferred
55: inflows of resources which represents an acquisition of net assets that applies to a future period(s)
55: and therefore, will not be recognized as an inflow of resources (revenue) until then. Included in this
55: category are TRS pension and OPEB. The Governmental Funds Balance Sheet also includes a
55: section for deferred inflows of resources. Unavailable revenue is reported as deferred inflows of
55: resources only in the Governmental Funds Balance Sheet.
55: NET POSITION AND FUND BALANCES
55: Government-wide Financial Statements
55: The District’s net position is composed of the following:
55: Net Investment in Capital Assets - the component of net position that reports capital assets less
55: both the accumulated depreciation and amortization and the outstanding balance of debt that is
55: directly attributable to the acquisition, construction, or improvement of these capital assets.
55: Restricted for Debt Service - the component of net position that reports the difference between
55: assets and liabilities of the Debt Service Fund adjusted on a government-wide basis that consists
55: of assets with constraints placed on their use by the bond covenants.
55: Restricted for Grants - the component of net position that reports the difference between assets
55: and liabilities of the Special Revenue Fund that consists of assets with constraints placed on their
55: use by the Department of Education, Department of Agriculture, TEA, and other grantor agencies
55: and organizations.
55: Unrestricted - the difference between the assets and liabilities that are not reported in net position
55: invested in capital assets, net position restricted for debt service, and net position restricted for grants.
55: The District applies restricted resources before unrestricted resources when an expense is incurred
55: for which restricted net position is available.
56: - 49 -
56: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
56: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
56: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
56: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2024
56: Governmental Fund Financial Statements
56: Inthefundfinancialstatements,governmentalfundsreportfundbalancesaseitheranon-spendable
56: fund balance or a spendable fund balance.
56: Non-spendable Fund Balance
56: Non-spendable fund balance is that portion of fund balance that is not expendable (such as
56: inventories) or is legally earmarked for a specific use. Non-spendable fund balance reserves
56: include inventories and prepaid items.
56: Spendable Fund Balance
56: Spendable fund balance is the portion of fund balance that is comprised of restricted, committed,
56: assigned, and unassigned fund balances.
56: Restricted Fund Balance - the component of the spendable fund balance constrained to a specific
56: purpose by the provider, such as a grantor. Restricted fund balance includes funds for the Nutrition
56: Services program, funds restricted for construction programs, funds used to retire debt, and
56: resources from other granting agencies.
56: Committed Fund Balance - the component of the spendable fund balance constrained to a specific
56: purpose by a resolution approved by the Board. Those committed amounts cannot be used for any
56: other purpose unless the Board removes or changes the specified use by taking the same action
56: used to establish it. Committed fund balance includes funds for potential litigation, claims, and
56: judgments.
56: Assigned Fund Balance - the component of the spendable fund balance that is earmarked for
56: a specific purpose by the Superintendent or Chief Financial Officer. The assigned amounts can
56: only be removed by the Superintendent or Chief Financial Officer. Assigned fund balance includes
56: funds for encumbrances which are purchase orders, contracts, and other commitments for the
56: expenditure of funds that are carried forward, insurance deductibles, specific program costs, and
56: other legal issues.
56: Unassigned Fund Balance - the component of the spendable fund balance within the General Fund
56: which may be spent for any legal purpose. This portion of the total fund balance in the general fund
56: is available to finance operating expenditures. Per Board Policy, the District’s unassigned fund
56: balance at year end shall equal at least three months of operating expenditures.
56: In general, it is the District’s policy to consider restricted resources to have been spent first when
56: an expenditure is incurred for purposes for which restricted and unrestricted (i.e. committed,
56: assigned, or unassigned) fund balances are available, followed by committed and then assigned
56: fund balances. Unassigned amounts are used only after the other resources have been used.

57: NOTE 2: HISD PUBLIC FACILITY CORPORATION
57: TheHoustonIndependentSchoolDistrictPublicFacilityCorporationisapublicnon-profitcorporation,
57: the creation of which was sponsored by the District pursuant to the Public Facility Corporation Act,
57: Article 717s, Vernon’s Texas Civil Statutes, as amended (the “Public Facility Corporation Act”).
57: The Public Facility Corporation Act authorizes the creation and utilization by school districts of
57: public facility corporations to issue bonds to provide for the acquisition, construction, rehabilitation,
57: renovation, repair, equipping, furnishing and placing in service of public facilities of its governmental
57: sponsor and further authorizes the governmental sponsor to incur lease purchase obligations in
57: favor of the corporation to serve as security for the bonds issued by the Public Facility Corporation.
57: To accomplish the financings, the Public Facility Corporation and the District have entered into a
57: number of “Leases With An Option to Purchase” pursuant to the terms of which the District will pay
57: to the Public Facility Corporation such lease payments at such times and in such amounts as will
57: be required to pay the principal and premium, if any, and interest on the lease revenue bonds as
57: they become due.
57: The Public Facility Corporation has utilized this structure to issue Lease Revenue Bonds for building
57: two high schools in 1998, a food warehouse facility in 2006, four elementary schools in 2010 and
57: three instructional facilities in 2015. The Public Facility Corporation refunded the Series 2006 (food
57: warehouse) bonds in fiscal year 2017 for the purpose of decreasing debt service costs in future
57: years. In fiscal year 2020 the Public Facility Corporation refunded the Series 2010B (BAB) bonds
57: for the purpose of decreasing debt service costs in future years. In fiscal year 2023, Series 2014A-
57: 1B bonds were refunded for the purpose of decreasing debt services costs in future years.

58: NOTE 3: CASH DEPOSITS AND INVESTMENTS
58: Statutes of the State of Texas and policies mandated by the District’s School Board authorize the
58: District to invest in obligations of the U.S. Government or its agencies, repurchase agreements,
58: commercial paper, corporate bonds, public fund investment pools, mutual funds and money market
58: accounts. All investments are held separately in each of the funds. All bank demand accounts are
58: recorded within the General Fund.
58: With the exception of one occasion, a three day period during the weekend of January 26 - 28, 2024,
58: District funds held at the depository bank were properly secured during the fiscal year ended June
58: 30, 2024. During January 26 - 28, 2024, District funds were undercollateralized by $3,511,381.
58: Cash, Demand and Time Deposits
58: As of June 30, 2024, the carrying amount of the District’s demand deposits and cash on hand was
58: $10,511,765. The $35,266,160 bank balance was fully covered on June 30, 2024 by a combination
58: offederaldepositinsurancethroughtheFDICandcollateralizationbysecuritiesheldbytheDistrict’s
58: depository in joint safekeeping at the Federal Reserve Bank in the District’s name.
58: The District purchased nonnegotiable certificates of deposit issued by Independent Bank and Unity
58: Bank, of $31,000,000 and $95,044, respectively, as of June 30, 2024, for a total of $31,095,044,
58: or 2.12 percent of the total investment portfolio. The collateral for the Independent Bank certificate
58: of deposit is comprised of Federal Home Loan Bank Letter of Credits in the amount of $13 million
58: issued with the District as beneficiary as well as $22.9 million of pledged U.S. agency securities.
58: Unity Bank’s certificate of deposit is covered in full by the FDIC; therefore, require no additional
58: collateral is required under the Public Funds Investment Act, Section 2257.022. Certificates of
58: deposit are reported at cost by the District.
58: Investments
58: The District’s investments in public funds investment pools and money market mutual funds at
58: June 30, 2024 include those with the Lone Star Investment Pool (“LSIP”), TexPool and Texas Fixed
58: Income Trust (TX-FIT). The value of the District’s position in the above pools is the same as the
58: value of the pool shares, which are measured at amortized cost or net asset value (NAV).
58: LSIPisapublicfundsinvestmentpoolcreatedpursuanttotheInterlocalCooperationActoftheState
58: of Texas, Article 4413(32c), Vernon’s Texas Civil Statutes, as amended, and is designed to provide
58: participating local governments with investment vehicles for local funds that may be required for
58: immediate expenditure. The primary objective of the LSIP is to emphasize the importance of safety
58: of principal and liquidity of pool assets.The administrator and distributor of LSIPis First Public, LLC,
58: a Texas limited liability company. Investment advisory services are provided by American Beacon
58: AdvisorsandMellonInvestmentsCorporation.ThecustodialaccountismanagedbyStateStreetBank.
58: The Lone Star Government Overnight Fund is a stable net asset value fund rated AAAm by
58: Standard & Poor’s rating service, Lone Star Corporate Overnight Fund is a stable net asset value
58: fund rated AAAm by Standard & Poor’s, and the Lone Star Corporate Overnight Plus Fund is a
58: stable net asset value rated AAAf/S1+ by Standard & Poor’s. All of these funds seek to maintain a
58: stable $1.00 net asset value.
58: The District’s investment in LSIP’s Corporate Overnight Fund, and the Corporate Overnight Plus
58: Fund,asofJune30,2024was$724,078and$766,186,929respectively,foratotalof$766,911,007,
58: representing 52.2 percent of the total portfolio.
58: TexPool is a public funds investment pool operated by the Texas Treasury Safekeeping Trust
58: Company, an entity of which the Texas Comptroller of Public Accounts is the sole officer, director,
58: and shareholder. Administrative and investment services are provided by Federated Hermes
58: TexPool and TexPool Prime funds are rated AAAm by Standard & Poor’s.
59: - 52 -
59: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
59: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
59: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
59: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2024
59: As of June 30, 2024, the District’s investment in TexPool Prime was $431,138,514, representing
59: 29.4 percent of the total portfolio.
59: TX-FIT is a public funds investment pool designed to provide local governments with investment
59: vehicles for local funds that may be required for immediate expenditure. It emphasizes safety
59: of principal and liquidity of pool assets. Texas FIT is managed by U.S. Fixed Income Trust. The
59: value of the District’s position in the pool is the same as the value of the pool shares, measured
59: at amortized cost or net asset value (NAV). The TX-FIT Pools are currently rated by Fitch Ratings.
59: The TX-FIT Cash Pool is currently rated AAAf/S1. The TX-FIT Government Pool is currently rated
59: AAAmmf.
59: The TX-FIT investments in each of the pools within the Trust are measured and reported at fair
59: market value using the guidelines established by Generally Accepted Accounting Principles
59: (GAAP). The Trust follows Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB) Accounting Standards
59: Topic (ASC) 820 Fair Value Measurement and Disclosure for financial reporting purposes.ASC 820
59: defines fair value, establishes a single framework for measuring fair value, and requires disclosures
59: about fair value measurement. TX-FIT does not meet all of the specific criteria outlined in GASB 79
59: paragraph 4, therefore TX-FIT investments are measured at fair value as provided in paragraph 11
59: of GASB Statement 31.
59: As of June 30, 2024, the District’s investment in Texas FIT was $140,866,867, representing 9.6
59: percent of the total portfolio.
59: GASB Statement No. 40, Deposit and Investment Risk Disclosures, an amendment to GASB
59: Statement No. 3, establishes and modifies disclosure requirements related to deposits and
59: investment risks: credit risk (including custodial credit risk and concentrations of credit risk), interest
59: rate risk, and foreign currency risk.
59: To ensure compliance with the various risk factors addressed in GASB Statement No. 40, the
59: Cash Management and Investment Policy is reviewed at least annually and presented to the
59: School Board for approval. The Investment Officers submit Quarterly Investment Reports to the
59: School Board certifying that all investments were purchased in compliance with the Public Fund
59: Investment Act.
59: The District’s Cash Management and Investment Policy contains the following objectives:
59: (1) assure the safety of District’s funds;
59: (2) maintain sufficient liquidity to provide adequate and timely working funds;
59: (3) maintenance of the public trust as custodians and managers responsible for the investing
59: of funds subject to state and federal laws;
59: (4) optimization of yield as expressed in terms of rate of return and interest earnings;
59: (5) diversification of investments as relating to maturity, instruments, and issuers shall be
59: considered a priority within the context of the overall investment policy; and
59: (6) avoid investments for speculation.
59: All objectives are approached with the judgment and care, under the circumstances then prevailing,
59: which persons of prudence, discretion, and intelligence exercise in the management of their own
59: affairs, not for speculation, but for investment, considering the safety of the capital, as well as
59: the probable income to be derived. Preservation of District capital is of highest importance. All
59: participants in the investment process seek to act responsibly as custodians of the public trust.
59: Investment officials avoid any transaction that might impair public confidence in the District’s ability
59: to govern effectively.

60: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2024
60: Interest Rate Risk
60: As a means of limiting its exposure to fair value losses that could occur from rising interest rates,
60: the District’s investment policy limits the maturities of investments and prohibits any investment for
60: speculative gains. The available funds in the General Fund may be invested for greater than one
60: year provided that all cash flow requirements have been met. Bond proceeds can be invested for a
60: period up to five years provided that the drawdown schedules permit such maturities.
60: The District recognizes that investment risks can result from market price changes to mitigate the
60: risk, all securities are purchased with the intention of holding to maturity. Portfolio diversification
60: is employed as a way to control risk. Investment officers are expected to display prudence in the
60: selection of securities, as a way to minimize risk. No individual investment transaction shall be
60: undertaken which jeopardizes the total capital position of the overall portfolio.
60: At June 30, 2024, the District’s exposure to interest rate risk as measured by segmented time
60: distribution by investment type, including fiduciary funds is summarized as follows:

60: 3. be issued for a specific face amount;
60: 4. be issued either through a direct placement or through broker dealers; and
60: 5. theDistrictwilldiversifyitsinvestmentincommercialpaperbyissuerandbylengthofmaturity.

61: GASB Statement Number 72 established a hierarchy that prioritizes the inputs used to measure fair
61: value. Certain investment types utilized by the District are not required to be measured at fair value.
61: The District has investments in TexPool, including both TexPool and TexPool Prime, which maintain
61: stable net asset values of $1 per share and utilize the amortized cost method as allowed by GASB
61: 79. The District has investments in Texas FIT which maintains a stable net asset value of $1 and
61: measures fair value as provided in paragraph 11 of GASB Statement 31. The District invests in
61: the Lone Star Corporate Overnight Fund which maintains a stable net asset value of $1 and the
61: underlying pool investments are reported at amortized cost per GASB 79. The District invests in the
61: Lone Star Corporate Overnight Plus Fund which maintains a stable net asset value of $1 and the
61: pool utilizes fair value reporting per GASB 72. The District has no unfunded commitments to these
61: pools and may redeem investments at any time.
61: CustodialCreditRisk-InvestmentspurchasedfortheDistrictaremadeinthenameoftheHouston
61: Independent School District. All investments are purchased with the delivery versus payment
61: method and are recorded in the District’s name on the Federal Reserve’s book entry system and
61: confirmed to the District via safekeeping receipt maintained on the books of the depository bank’s
61: safekeeping department.
61: Concentration of Credit Risk - The investment portfolio is diversified to eliminate the risk of loss
61: resulting from over concentration of assets in a specific maturity, a specific issue, or a specific class
61: of securities. Within the cash flow requirement constraints, investment maturities are staggered in
61: a way that avoids undue concentration of assets in a specific maturity sector.
61: The District does not invest its portfolio in any single investment pool. Funds are subdivided
61: between various pools for diversification and security reasons.
61: The District is prohibited from investing in the following types of investments:
61: a. obligations whose payment represents the coupon payments on the outstanding principal
61: balance of the underlying mortgage-backed security collateral and pays no principal;
61: b. obligations whose payment represents the principal stream of cash flow from the underlying
61: mortgage-backed security collateral and bears no interest;
61: c. collateralized mortgage obligations that have a stated final maturity date of greater than 10
61: years; and
61: d. collateralized mortgage obligations whose interest rate is determined by an index that adjusts
61: opposite to the changes in a market index.

62: NOTE 4: PROPERTY TAXES AND STATE AID REVENUE
62: PROPERTY TAXES
62: The appraisal of property within the District is the responsibility of the Harris County Appraisal
62: District (“Appraisal District”). The District’s ad valorem property tax is levied annually in October
62: on the assessed value as of the prior January 1 for all real and business personal property located
62: in the District. Taxes are due on receipt of the tax bill and are delinquent if not paid by January
62: 31 of the next calendar year. Such taxes are applicable to the fiscal year in which they are levied
62: and become delinquent with an enforceable lien on property after January 31 of the subsequent
62: calendar year. Delinquent taxes receivable on the government-wide statement of net position and
62: the governmental fund balance sheet is net of estimated uncollectible taxes.
62: For the current fiscal year, the Board of Education set a tax rate of $0.8683 per $100 of assessed
62: valuation. The maintenance and debt service portions of such rate are $0.7016 and $0.1667
62: respectively. The 2023 tax year assessed valuation was $229,523,362,674 which resulted in a
62: gross tax levy of $1,992,951,588 for the current fiscal year. After deductions of all exemptions
62: and reductions provided by law and those granted by the District, the 2023 tax year levy was
62: $1,931,098,418 as of June 30, 2024.
62: Property taxes which are measurable (quantifiable) and available (collectible within the current
62: period or soon enough thereafter to finance expenditures of the current period, which the District
62: has estimated to be collected in the two months after the fiscal year end) are recognized as
62: revenue in the year of levy in the governmental fund financial statements. Property taxes, which are
62: measurable but not available, are recorded net of estimated uncollectible amounts, as unavailable
62: revenues in the year of the levy in the governmental financial statements. Such unavailable
62: revenues are recognized in the fund financial statements as revenue in the fiscal year in which they
62: become available. In the government-wide financial statements, property taxes are recognized
62: as revenues in the year for which the taxes are levied. Uncollectible property taxes in the current
62: period increased the allowance for uncollectible property taxes in the government-wide and fund
62: financial statements by $13,435,788.

62: fund financial statements as of June 30, 2024 are as follows:

69: Transfers areusedto(1)moverevenues fromthefund thatstatute or budget requires tocollect
69: them to the fund that statute or budget requires to expend them, (2) move receipts restricted to
69: debt service from the funds collecting the receipts to the debt service fund as debt service
69: payments become due, (3) use unrestricted revenues collected in the general fund to finance
69: various programs accounted for in other funds in accordance with budgetary authorizations,
69: and (4) use unrestricted revenues collected in a proprietary fund to finance activities in the
69: general fund or another proprietary fund.
69: NOTE 8: LONG-TERM LIABILITIES AND RELATED DEFERRED INFLOWS
69: AND OUTFLOWS
69: The District’s long-term liabilities consist of bond and note indebtedness, leases and
69: subscriptions, health insurance, workers’ compensation, compensated absences, net pension
69: liability and net OPEB liability. The current requirements for general obligation bonds principal
69: and interest expenditures are accounted for in the debt service fund. Current funding
69: requirements for leases, subscriptions, health insurance, and workers’ compensation are
69: accounted for in the governmental and proprietary funds. Pension and OPEB long-term
69: liabilities are generally liquidated with resources of the general fund.
69: Transfers are used to (1) move revenues from the fund that statute or budget requires to collect
69: them to the fund that statute or budget requires to expend them, (2) move receipts restricted
69: to debt service from the funds collecting the receipts to the debt service fund as debt service
69: payments become due, (3) use unrestricted revenues collected in the general fund to finance
69: various programs accounted for in other funds in accordance with budgetary authorizations, and
69: (4) use unrestricted revenues collected in a proprietary fund to finance activities in the general fund
69: or another proprietary fund.
69: NOTE 8: LONG-TERM LIABILITIES AND RELATED DEFERRED INFLOWS
69: AND OUTFLOWS
69: The District’s long-term liabilities consist of bond and note indebtedness, leases and subscriptions,
69: health insurance, workers’ compensation, compensated absences, net pension liability and
69: net OPEB liability. The current requirements for general obligation bonds principal and interest
69: expenditures are accounted for in the debt service fund. Current funding requirements for leases,
69: subscriptions, health insurance, and workers’ compensation are accounted for in the governmental
69: and proprietary funds. Pension and OPEB long-term liabilities are generally liquidated with
69: resources of the general fund.

70: $1,592,255 of Internal Service Funds’ compensated absences payable and $17,684,494 of claims

70: claims and judgments, and compensated absences are generally liquidated by the General Fund.
70: Debt Payable-Governmental Activities
70: At June 30, 2024, the District had $1,813,555,000 outstanding of general obligation and lease
70: revenue bonds, and maintenance tax notes. These debt obligations were issued at various dates
70: from November 1, 2009, through March 21, 2023, with interest rates ranging from 1.00 percent to
70: 6.17 percent, for the purpose of new construction, and equipment of facilities and maintenance of
70: facilities. All debt obligations fully mature at various dates through February 15, 2043. As of June
70: 30, 2024, the District had no authorized but unissued debt capacity.
70: Of the District’s $1,813,555,000 total debt outstanding at June 30, 2024, $32,840,000 of bonds
70: payable are attributable to lease revenue bonds issued by the Public Facility Corporation.
70: In current and prior years, the District legally defeased certain bonds by placing cash and/or
70: proceeds of refunding bond issues in an irrevocable trust to provide for all future debt service
70: payments on the refunded debt. Accordingly, the trust account assets and the liability for the
70: defeased bonds are not included in the District’s financial statements. As of June 30, 2024, there
70: were no outstanding defeased bonds.

71: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
71: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2024
71: On May 30, 2024, the District defeased $40,450,500 in outstanding par on the Variable Rate Limited
71: Tax Schoolhouse Bonds, Series 2013B, that were sold with a final maturity date of June 1, 2036.
71: The District utilized available debt service funds for the full redemption of the bond series.
71: Also, on May 30, 2024, the District defeased $68,720,000 of outstanding par on the Limited Tax
71: Refunding Bonds, Series 2014B using available cash resources from the debt service fund. The
71: Limited Tax Refunding Bonds, Series 2014B were originally issued on December 16, 2014, in a par
71: amount of $365,395,000.
71: During the fiscal year ended June 30, 2024, the District did not issue any new debt or engage in
71: any refunding activities. Consequently, there are no new debt service savings or economic gains
71: to report for this period.
71: Premiums or discounts on each bond transaction are amortized over the life of the bonds. Pursuant
71: to GASB 65 underwriter’s discounts and issuance costs have been expensed in the period reported.
71: The District issues variable rate debt for term periods and interest rate modes which may be
71: changed should the District elect to do so at remarketing dates. The maximum rate of the variable
71: rate debt cannot exceed the maximum net effective interest rate permitted under Chapter 1204
71: Texas Government Code, as amended. Following the new rate periods, the bonds will convert to
71: and bear interest at a term rate to be determined by the remarketing agent. Subsequent to the new
71: rate periods, the interest rate modes may be changed to a weekly rate, monthly rate, quarterly
71: rate, semiannual rate, flexible rate or converted to a fixed rate until stated maturity. The Bonds are
71: subject to mandatory tender at the end of the new rate period. In the event of a failed remarketing
71: for any series, a step rate of 7.00 percent (Series 2014A-2, Series 2023C) will be invoked until such
71: time as the bonds are successfully remarketed.Afailed remarketing will not be considered an event
71: of default.

74: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
74: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2024
74: Other long-term debt
74: Federal Arbitrage - The Tax Reform Act of 1986 instituted certain arbitrage restrictions consisting
74: of complex regulations with respect to issuance of tax-exempt bond proceeds at an interest yield
74: greater than the interest yield paid to bondholders. Generally, all interest paid to bondholders can
74: be retroactively rendered taxable if applicable rebates are not reported and paid to the Internal
74: Revenue Service at least every five years for applicable bond issues. Accordingly, there is the risk
74: that if such calculations are not performed or are not performed correctly, a liability to the District
74: could result. The District periodically engages an arbitrage consultant to perform the calculations in
74: accordance with the Internal Revenue Service’s rules and regulations. The District did not identify
74: arbitrage liability as of June 30, 2024.
74: Compensated Absences - On retirement or death of certain employees hired before October 10,
74: 1972, the District pays any accumulated unused sick leave.Additionally, the District may “buy back”
74: any accrued but unused state leave from employees in accordance with the Attendance Incentive
74: Plan. Also, certain employees are entitled to receive accrued vacation pay in a lump-sum cash
74: payment upon termination of employment with the District. The General Fund has typically been
74: used in prior years to liquidate compensated absences.
74: NOTE 9: LEASES
74: The District has entered into multiple lease agreements as lessee. The leases allow the right-to-use
74: computers, copiers and other assets over the term of the lease. The District is required to make
74: annual payments on the laptop leases and monthly payments on all other leases at its incremental
74: borrowing rate or the interest rate stated or implied within the leases. The lease rate, term and
74: ending lease liability are as follows:

78: Property, Casualty, General and Professional Liability
78: The District purchasesAll-Risk Property Insurance with limits of $250,000,000. The policies include
78: sublimits of $100,000,000 per occurrence for flood, $75,000,000 for quake and $150,000,000 per
78: occurrence for Named Storms. General and Professional Liability risks are insured with limits of
78: $9,750,000. Within these policy limits, the District’s exposure for covered losses is limited to the
78: policy deductibles and self-insured retentions. Automobile liability exposures are self-insured in
78: Texas and insurance coverage is purchased for out of state risks with a $1,000,000 limit. The
78: District is self-insured for workers compensation and employer’s liability risk exposure. The District
78: has not had any claims in excess of its policy limits in the past three years.
78: NOTE 12: EMPLOYEES’ RETIREMENT PLAN
78: Pensions
78: Plan Description
78: The District participates in a cost-sharing multiple-employer defined benefit pension that has a
78: special funding situation. The plan is administered by the Teacher Retirement System of Texas
78: (TRS). It is a defined benefit pension plan established and administered in accordance with the
78: Texas Constitution, Article XVI, Section 67 and Texas Government Code, Title 8, Subtitle C. The
78: pension trust fund is a qualified pension trust under Section 401(a) of the Internal Revenue Code.
78: The Texas Legislature establishes benefits and contribution rates within the guidelines of the
78: Texas Constitution. The pension’s Board of Trustees does not have the authority to establish or
78: amend benefit terms. All employees of public, state-supported educational institutions in Texas
78: who are employed for one-half or more of the standard workload and who are not exempted from
78: membership under Texas Government Code, Title 8, Section 822.002 are covered by the system.
78: Pension Plan Fiduciary Net Position
78: Detail information about the Teacher Retirement System’s Fiduciary Net Position is available in a
78: separately issued Annual Comprehensive Financial Report that includes financial statements and
78: required supplementary information. That report may be obtained by visiting https://www.trs.texas.
78: gov/Pages/about_publications.aspx; by writing to the TRS at 1000 Red River Street, Austin, Texas
78: 78701-2698; or by calling (512) 542-6592.
78: The information in the Notes to the Financial Statements of the TRS 2023 Annual Comprehensive
78: Financial Report provides the Pension Plan fiduciary net position for the year ending August 31,
78: 2023 as follow:
78: Net Pension Liability Total
78: Total Pension Liability $ 255,860,886,500
78: Less: Plan FiduciaryNet Position 187,170,535,558

79: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
79: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
79: NOTES TO THE BASIC FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
79: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2024
79: Benefit Provisions and Service Requirements
79: TRS administers retirement, disability, and death and survivor benefits to employees and
79: beneficiaries of employees of the public school system of Texas. The pension formula is calculated
79: using 2.3 percent times the average of the five highest annual creditable salaries times years of
79: creditedservicetoarriveattheannualstandardannuityexceptformemberswhoaregrandfathered,
79: the three highest annual salaries are used. Service requirements are as follows:
79: Normal -- Age 65 with 5 years of credited service, or when the sum of member’s age and years of
79: credit equals or exceeds 80.
79: Reduced --Age 55 with at least 5 years of credited service, or earlier than 55 with 30 or more years
79: of credited service.
79: Members are fully vested after five years of creditable service and are entitled to any benefit for
79: which eligibility requirements have been met.There are additional provisions for reduced retirement
79: if the sum of the member’s age and years of service credit total at least 80, but the member is
79: less than age 60 or 62 depending on date of employment, or if the member was grandfathered
79: under a previous rule. For more detail about benefits see the Summary of Benefits section of TRS
79: 2023 Annual Comprehensive Financial Report. There are no automatic post-employment benefit
79: changes, including automatic COLAs. Ad hoc post-employment benefit changes, including ad hoc
79: COLAs can be granted by the Texas Legislature as noted in the Plan description.
79: Texas Government Code section 821.006 prohibits benefit improvements, if, as a result of the
79: particular action, the time required to amortizeTRS unfunded actuarial liabilities would be increased
79: to a period that exceeds 31 years, or, if the amortization period already exceeds 31 years, the
79: period would be increased by such action. Actuarial implications of the funding provided in the
79: manner are determined by the System’s actuary.
79: Contributions
79: Contribution requirements are established or amended pursuant to Article 16, section 67 of the
79: Texas Constitution which requires the Texas legislature to establish a member contribution rate
79: of not less than 6 percent of the member’s annual compensation and a state contribution of not
79: less than 6 percent and not more than 10 percent of the aggregate annual compensation paid to
79: members of the system during the fiscal year.
79: Employee contribution rates are set in state statute, Texas Government Code 825.402. The TRS
79: Pension Reform Bill (Senate Bill 12) of the 86th Texas Legislature amended Texas Government
79: Code 825.402 for member contributions and increased employee and employer contribution rates
79: for fiscal years 2020 through 2025.
79: 2023 2024
79: Member 8.00% 8.25%
79: Non-Employer Contributing Entity (State) 8.00% 8.25%
79: Employers 8.00% 8.25%
79: District's 2024 District Contributions $ 72,224,501
79: District's 2024 Member Contributions $ 131,002,332
79: District's 2024 NECE On-Behalf Contributions $ 84,454,248
79: Contributors to the plan include members, employers, and the State of Texas as the only non-
79: employer contributing entity. The State contributes to the plan in accordance with state statutes and
79: the General Appropriations Act (GAA).

80: by the amounts described below which are paid by the District. The District is required to pay the
80: employer contribution rate in the following instances:
80: • On the portion of the member’s salary that exceeds the statutory minimum for members
80: entitled to the statutory minimum under Section 21.402 of the Texas Education Code
80: • During a new member’s first 90 days of employment
80: • When any part or all of an employee’s salary is paid by federal funding source, a privately
80: sponsored source, from non-educational and general, or local funds
80: In addition to the employer contributions listed above, the District is required to pay the following
80: surcharges:
80: • 1.8 percent of the member’s salary beginning in fiscal year 2023, gradually increasing to
80: 2 percent in fiscal year 2025
80: • When employing a retiree of the Teacher Retirement System, the District shall pay both the
80: member contribution and the state contribution as an employment after retirement surcharge

81: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2024
81: Discount Rate and Long-term Expected Rate of Return
81: A single discount rate of 7.00 percent was used to measure the total pension liability. The single
81: discount rate was based on the expected rate of return on pension plan investments of 7.00 percent.
81: The projection of cash flows used to determine the single discount rate assumed that contributions
81: from active members, employers and the non-employer contributing entity will be made at the
81: statutorily required rates set during the 2019 session. It is assumed that future employer and state
81: contributions will be 9.50 percent of payroll in fiscal year 2024 increasing to 9.56 percent of in fiscal
81: year 2025 and thereafter. This includes all employer and state contributions for active and rehired
81: retirees. Based on these assumptions, the pension plan’s fiduciary net position was projected to
81: be available to make all future benefit payments of current plan members. Therefore, the long-
81: term expected rate of return on pension plan investments was applied to all periods of projected
81: benefit payments to determine the total pension liability. The long-term rate of return on pension
81: plan investments was determined using a building-block method in which best estimates ranges
81: of expected future real rates of return (expected returns, net of pension plan investment expense
81: and inflation) are developed for each major asset class. These ranges are combined to produce
81: the long-term expected rate of return by weighing the expected future real rates of return by the
81: target asset allocation percentage and by adding expected inflation. Best estimates of geometric
81: real rates of return for each major asset class included in TRS’s target asset allocation as ofAugust
81: 31, 2023 are summarized below:

83: Changes since the Prior Measurement Date
83: The actuarial assumptions and methods are the same as used in the determination of prior year’s
83: net pension liability.
83: The Texas 2023 Legislature passed legislation that provides a one-time stipend to certain retired
83: teachers. The stipend was paid to retirees beginning in September of 2023. The Legislature
83: appropriated funds to pay for this one-time stipend so there will be no impact on the Net Position
83: Liability of TRS. In addition, the Legislature also provided for a cost of living adjustment (COLA) to
83: retirees which was approved during the November 2023 election which was paid January 2024.

83: 2023.
83: Other Post-Employment Benefit Plans
83: Health Care Plan Description (TRS-Care)
83: The District participates in the Texas Public School Retired Employees Group Insurance Program
83: (TRS- Care). It is a multiple-employer, cost-sharing defined Other Post-Employment Benefit
83: (OPEB) plan that has a special funding situation. The plan is administered through a trust by the
83: Teacher Retirement System of Texas (TRS) Board of Trustees. It is established and administered
83: in accordance with the Texas Insurance Code, Chapter 1575. The Board of Trustees is granted
83: the authority to establish basic and optional group insurance coverage for participants as well as
83: to amend benefit terms as needed under Chapter 1575.052. The Board may adopt rules, plans,
83: procedures, and orders reasonably necessary to administer the program, including minimum
83: benefits and financing standards.

85: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2024
85: Contributions
85: Contribution rates for the TRS-Care plan are established in state statute by the Texas Legislature,
85: and there is no continuing obligation to provide benefits beyond each fiscal year. The TRS-Care
85: plan is currently funded on a pay-as-you-go basis and is subject to change based on available
85: funding. Funding for TRS-Care is provided by retiree premium contributions and contributions from
85: the state, active employees, and school districts based upon public school district payroll. The TRS
85: Board of trustees does not have the authority to set or amend contribution rates.
85: Texas Insurance Code, section 1575.202 establishes the state’s contribution rate which is 1.25
85: percent of the employee’s salary. Section 1575.203 establishes the active employee’s rate which is
85: 0.65percentofpay.Section1575.204establishesanemployercontributionrateofnotlessthan0.25
85: percent or not more than 0.75 percent of the salary of each active employee of the public school.
85: The actual employer contribution rate is prescribed by the Legislature in the GeneralAppropriations
85: Act. The following table shows contributions to the TRS-Care plan by type of contributor.
85: 2023 2024

85: Contribution Rates
85: In addition to the employer contributions listed above, there is an additional surcharge District
85: is subject to. When the District hires a TRS retiree, it is required to pay to TRS Care a monthly

88: Medicare Part D
88: The Medicare Modernization Act of 2003 (MMA) created an outpatient prescription drug benefit
88: program known as Medicare Part D. The Texas Public School Retired Employee Group Insurance
88: Program (TRS-Care) is offering a Medicare Part D Plan and is participating in the Retiree Drug
88: Subsidy plan for eligible TRS-Care participants. For the years ended August 31, 2024 and August
88: 31, 2023, the subsidy payments received by TRS-Care on behalf of the District were $8,080,397
88: and $7,150,321, respectively. TRS issues a publicly available financial report. That report may be
88: found by visiting the TRS Web site at www.trs.texas.gov/Pages/re_gasb_24.aspx.
88: NOTE 13: COMMITMENTS AND CONTINGENCIES
88: The District received significant financial assistance from numerous federal and state governmental
88: agenciesintheformofgrants.Thedisbursementsoffundsreceivedundertheseprogramsgenerally
88: requires compliance with terms and conditions specified in the grant agreements and are subject
88: to audit by the grantor agencies and the Texas Education Agency. Any disallowed claims resulting
88: from such audits could become a liability of the General Fund.
88: The District is party to various other legal actions, none of which is believed by the administration
88: or legal counsel to have a material effect on the financial condition of the District. Accordingly,
88: no provision for losses has been recorded in the accompanying financial statements for such
88: contingencies.
88: NOTE 14: NONMONETARY TRANSACTIONS
88: During fiscal year 2024, the District received goods purchased by the State of Texas for the
88: benefit of the District through the Instructional Materials and Technology Allotment program. The
88: instructional materials have been recorded in the amount $80 thousand in the special revenue fund
88: as both state revenues and expenditures, which represent the amount of consideration given by
88: the State of Texas.
88: Approximately twenty-six middle schools and high schools were selected to participate in the
88: Verizon Innovative Learning School (VILS), the education initiative of Verizon. Verizon awarded
88: data plans to the District in fiscal year 2023. In-kind donations in the amount of approximately $1.3
88: million have been recorded in the special revenue fund as both local revenues and expenditures.
88: The program was terminated effective November 17, 2023.

96: NOTES TO REQUIRED SUPPLEMENTARY INFORMATION
96: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2024
96: Note 1: Stewardship, Compliance, and Accountability
96: The District uses the following procedures in establishing the budgets reflected in the financial statements:
96: • Prior to June 30th of the preceding fiscal year, the District prepares a budget for the next succeeding
96: fiscal year beginning July 1. The operating budget includes proposed expenditures and the means of
96: financing them.
96: • Public hearings are conducted to obtain citizens' comments. However, the final priorities and funding
96: of projects are determined by the Board of Managers that establishes a tax rate to support the
96: approved budget.
96: • A meeting of the Board of Managers is then called for the purpose of adopting the proposed budget.
96: • Budgets are approved on a fund-by-fund basis. The District can make amendments to the budget at
96: any time during the year; however, amendments changing functional expenditure categories (as
96: defined in the Texas Education Agency’s Financial Accountability System Resource Guide) must be
96: ratified by the Board of Managers. Budget amendments are presented to the Board of Managers
96: monthly or as needed.
96: • Budgets are considered a management control and planning tool and, as such, are incorporated into
96: the accounting system of the District.
96: • Annual budgets are legally adopted for the General Fund, the Debt Service Fund, and the Nutrition
96: Services Fund. The budgets are prepared on a basis of accounting that is used for reporting in
96: accordance with generally accepted accounting principles and are monitored by the Budget and
96: Financial Planning Office. By state law, expenditures may not legally exceed budgeted
96: appropriations, as amended, at the function level by fund. Unexpended appropriations lapse at the
96: end of the fiscal year.
96: • Per TEA requirements, the expenditure in any functional category should not exceed the approved
96: budget. For the year ended June 30, 2024:
96: General Fund, Function 21, Instructional Leadership exceeded budget due to payroll costs
96: General Fund, Function 23, School Leadership exceeded budget due to payroll costs
96: General Fund, Function 71, Debt Service exceeded budget due to SBITA payments.
96: Nutrition Services Fund, Function 51, Plant Maintenance and Operations exceeded budget due to
96: payroll costs
96: Nutrition Services Fund, Function 71, Debt Service exceeded budget due to SBITA payments
96: Notes to Required Supplementary Information
96: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
96: NOTES TO REQUIRED SUPPLEMENTARY INFORMATION
96: FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDED JUNE 30, 2024

97: Net Pension Liability
97: Changes in demographic and economic assumptions
97: For Measurement Date August 31, 2018 – Net Pension Liability and Net OPEB Liability:
97: - Demographic assumptions including post-retirement mortality, termination rates, and rates of retirement
97: and economic assumptions, including rates of salary increase for individual participants were updated
97: based on the experience study performed for TRS for the period ending August 31, 2017.
97: Changes in benefit terms
97: For Measurement Date August 31, 2023 – Net Pension Liability:
97: - One-time stipend was paid to certain retired teachers in September of 2023 as per legislation passed in
97: 2023. Additionally, cost of living adjustment (COLA) was provided to retirees in January 2024. These
97: payments have no impact on the Net Pension Liability as of August 31,2023.
97: For Measurement Date August 31, 2018 – Net OPEB Liability:
97: - Changes of benefit terms were made effective September 1,2017 by the 85th Texas Legislature.
97: Other changes
97: For Measurement Date August 31, 2022 – Net OPEB Liability:
97: - The participation rate for pre-65 retirees was lowered from 65% to 62%. The participation rate for post-
97: 65 retirees was lowered from 40% to 25%.
97: For Measurement Date August 31, 2020 – Net OPEB Liability:
97: - The participation rate for post-65 retirees was lowered from 50% to 40%.
97: - The ultimate health care trend rate assumption decreased to reflect the repeal of the excise (Cadillac) tax
97: on high-cost employer health plans.
97: For Measurement Date August 31, 2019 – Net Pension Liability:
97: - With the enactment of SB3 by the 2019 Texas Legislature, as assumption was made about how this
97: would impact future salaries. It is assumed that eligible active members will each receive a $2,700
97: increase in fiscal year 2020. This is in addition to the salary increase expected based on the actuarial
97: assumptions.
97: For Measurement Date August 31, 2019 – Net OPEB Liability:
97: - The participation rate for pre-65 retirees was lowered from 70% to 65%. The participation rate for post-
97: 65 retirees was lowered from 75% to 50%. 25% of pre-65 retirees are assumed to discontinue their
97: coverage at age 65.
98: - 90 -
98: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
98: - The trend rates were reset to better reflect the plan’s anticipated experience.
98: - The percentage of retirees who are assumed to have two-person coverage was lowered from 20% to
98: 15%. In addition, the participation assumption for the surviving spouses of employees that die while
98: actively employed was lowered from 20% to 10%.
98: For Measurement Date August 31, 2018 – Net OPEB Liability:
98: - Adjustments were made for retirees that were known to have discontinued their health care coverage in
98: fiscal year 2018.
98: - The health care trend rate assumption was updated to reflect the anticipated return of the Health Insurer
98: Fee (HIF) in 2020.

100: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
100: Nonmajor Enterprise Funds
100: NONMAJOR ENTERPRISE FUNDS
100: NONMAJOR ENTERPRISE FUNDS
100: The Medicaid Fund is used to account for the financial operations of the district’s school-based federal Medicaid
100: program that provides health and medical services to Medicaid eligible special education students. In addition,
100: the district currently provides consulting services and Medicaid claims management services to approximately
100: 120 Texas school districts.
100: The Marketplace Fund is used to account for the financial operations of the Starbucks® coffee and snack bar
100: located on the first level of the Hattie Mae White (HMW) Educational Support Center. The district established this
100: Marketplace as a benefit to employees working in the HMW building. The Starbucks® coffee and snack bar
100: provides a variety Starbucks® drinks and grab-and-go items made available to guests and employees at HMW.

104: - 95 -
104: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
104: Internal Service Funds
104: INTERNAL SERVICE FUNDS
104: The Print Shop Fund is used to account for all the printing and distribution activities of the District’s two print shop
104: facilities. Schools and departments are charged for the costs of printing and distribution requests. During non-peak
104: times, the Print Shop contracts with other governmental agencies requiring assistance during their peak periods. In
104: addition, the Print Shop handles the districtwide copier contract/services for all schools and departments.
104: The Health Insurance Fund is used to account for the health insurance plan administered by the District. The
104: plan is a self-insured plan requiring the District and its employees to contribute the cost. The District contributes
104: approximately 81.87 percent of the annual cost of the plan with employees paying for the remaining 18.13 percent.
104: These contributions are paid from all governmental and proprietary funds to the Health Insurance Fund from which
104: all claims and administrative expenses are paid.
104: The Workers’ Compensation Fund is used to account for risk financing activities related to the self-insured
104: workers’ compensation program. All employees of the District are covered by this plan for injuries occurring on the
104: job. The District contributes 100 percent of the funding for this program.
104: The Internal Services Fund is used to account for the fee-for-services offered by the District to serve the specific
104: needs of schools and departments. TheAlternative Certification,Athletics, UIL, and Shared Services programs are
104: accounted for within this fund.

114: STATISTICAL INFORMATION
114: (Unaudited)
114: The Statistical Section of the District’s annual comprehensive financial report presents detailed information as a
114: context for understanding what the information in the financial statements, note disclosures, and required
114: supplementary information says about the District’s overall financial health.
114: CONTENTS
114: Financial Trends
114: These schedules contain information to help the reader understand how the District’s financial performance has
114: changed over time.
114: Revenue Capacity
114: These schedules contain information to help the reader assess the District’s major revenue sources.
114: Debt Capacity
114: These schedules contain information to help the reader assess the affordability of the District’s current levels of
114: outstanding debt and the District’s ability to issue additional debt in the future.
114: Demographic and Economic Information
114: These schedules offer demographic and economic indicators to help the reader understand the environment
114: within which the District’s financial activities take place.
114: Operating Information
114: These schedules contain service and infrastructure data to help the reader understand how the information in
114: the district’s financial report relates to the services the District provides and activities it performs.
114: SOURCES
114: Unless otherwise noted, the information in these schedules is derived from the annual comprehensive financial
114: reports for the relevant year.

122: (1) The payment to escrow agents - current refundings in fiscal years 2015, 2016, 2017, 2019, 2020, 2021, 2022, and 2023 have been deducted from non-capital expenditures in order to insure comparability between
122: years, as these amounts are remarketing of variable interest debt, not a true debt refunding.
122: (2) Debt service ratio is equal to (Principal+Interest+payments to escrow agents)/ (Total Expenditures-Total Capital Assets Being Depreciated - as reported in the Reconciliation of the Governmental Funds Statement

122: Revenues, Expenditures, and Changes in Fund Balances to the Statement of Activities.
122: The ratio of 18.47% was restated from the original 19.59% published in fiscal year 2019.

132: Local Excess Revenue (Recapture)
132: - 95 -
132: H OUSTON I N DE PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
132: Local
132: Excess
132: Fiscal Year Revenue
132: 2017 $ 91,544,985
132: 2018 $ 168,325,648
132: 2019 $ 185,111,161
132: 2020 $ 74,910,822 *
132: 2021 $ 198,143,888
132: 2022 $ 186,526,718
132: 2023 $ 275,327,222
132: 2024 $ - **
132: In fiscal year 2017, Houston ISD began sending "local excess revenue" to the state.
132: The district is considered property rich and is required to send back local tax revenue
132: in excess of entitlement.
132: Chapter 49 of the Texas Education Code (TEC) makes provisions for certain school districts to
132: share their local tax revenue with other school districts. These provisions are sometimes referred
132: to as "share the wealth", "Robin Hood" or "recapture" because recaptured funds are redistributed
132: by the school finance system.
132: * Decrease in local excess revenue payment due to impact of HB3 which revised the formula
132: ** No payment required due to tax compression and $100k homestead exemption

170: Independent Auditor’s Report on Internal Control over Financial Reporting and on Compliance and Other Matters Based on an Audit of Financial Statements Performed in Accordance with
170: Government Auditing Standards
170: Weaver and Tidwell, L.L.P.
170: 4400 Post Oak Parkway Suite 1100 | Houston, Texas 77027
170: Main: 713.850.8787
170: CPAs AND ADVISORS | WEAVER.COM 151
170: Independent Auditor’s Report on Internal Control over Financial
170: Reporting and on Compliance and Other Matters Based on an
170: Audit of Financial Statements Performed in Accordance
170: with Government Auditing Standards
170: To the School Board of
170: Houston Independent School District
170: We have audited, in accordance with auditing standards generally accepted in the United States of
170: America and the standards applicable to financial audits contained in Government Auditing Standards
170: issued by the Comptroller General of the United States, the financial statements of the governmental
170: activities, business-type activities, each major fund, and the aggregate remaining fund information of
170: Houston Independent School District (the District) as of and for the year ended June 30, 2024, and the
170: related notes to the financial statements, which collectively comprise the District’s basic financial
170: statements, and have issued our report thereon dated November 7, 2024.
170: Report on Internal Control over Financial Reporting
170: In planning and performing our audit of the financial statements, we considered the District’s internal
170: control over financial reporting (internal control) to determine the audit procedures that are
170: appropriate in the circumstances for the purpose of expressing our opinions on the financial statements,
170: but not for the purpose of expressing an opinion on the effectiveness of the District’s internal control.
170: Accordingly, we do not express an opinion on the effectiveness of the District’s internal control.
170: A deficiency in internal control exists when the design or operation of a control does not allow
170: management or employees in the normal course of performing their assigned functions, to prevent, or
170: detect and correct misstatements on a timely basis. A material weakness is a deficiency, or a
170: combination of deficiencies, in internal control, such that there is a reasonable possibility that a material
170: misstatement of the entity’s financial statements will not be prevented, or detected and corrected on a
170: timely basis. A significant deficiency is a deficiency, or a combination of deficiencies, in internal control
170: that is less severe than a material weakness, yet important enough to merit attention by those charged
170: with governance.
170: Our consideration of internal control over financial reporting was for the limited purpose described in
170: the first paragraph of this section and was not designed to identify all deficiencies in internal control that
170: might be material weaknesses or significant deficiencies. Given these limitations, during our audit we did
170: not identify any deficiencies in internal control that we consider to be material weaknesses. However,
170: material weaknesses or significant deficiencies may exist that were not identified.
170: Report on Compliance and Other Matters
170: As part of obtaining reasonable assurance about whether the District’s financial statements are free
170: from material misstatement, we performed tests of its compliance with certain provisions of laws,
170: regulations, contracts, and grant agreements, noncompliance with which could have a direct and
170: material effect on the financial statements. However, providing an opinion on compliance with those
170: provisions was not an objective of our audit and, accordingly, we do not express such an opinion. The
170: results of our tests disclosed no instances of noncompliance or other matters that are required to be
170: reported under Government Auditing Standards.
171: - 154 -
171: The School Board of
171: Houston Independent School District
171: 152
171: Purpose of this Report
171: The purpose of this report is solely to describe the scope of our testing of internal control and
171: compliance and the results of that testing, and not to provide an opinion on the effectiveness of the
171: entity’s internal control or on compliance. This report is an integral part of an audit performed in
171: accordance with Government Auditing Standards in considering the entity’s internal control and
171: compliance. Accordingly, this communication is not suitable for any other purpose.

171: Houston, Texas

172: Independent Auditor’s Report on Compliance for Each Major Federal and State
172: Program and Report on Internal Control over Compliance
172: Required by the Uniform Guidance and Texas Grant Management Standards
172: To the School Board of
172: Houston Independent School District
172: Report on Compliance for Each Major Federal Program and State Program
172: Opinion on Each Major Federal and State Program
172: We have audited Houston Independent School District’s (the District) compliance with the types of
172: compliance requirements described in the U.S. Office of Management and Budget (OMB) Compliance
172: Supplement and the Texas Grant Management Standards that could have a direct and material effect
172: on each of the District’s major federal and state programs for the year ended June 30, 2024. The
172: District’s major federal and state programs are identified in the summary of auditor’s results section of
172: the accompanying schedule of findings and questioned costs.
172: In our opinion, the District complied, in all material respects, with the compliance requirements referred
172: to above that could have a direct and material effect on each of its major federal and state programs
172: for the year ended June 30, 2024.
172: Basis for Opinion on Each Major Federal and State Program
172: We conducted our audit of compliance in accordance with auditing standards generally accepted
172: in the United States of America (GAAS); the standards applicable to financial audits contained
172: in Government Auditing Standards issued by the Comptroller General of the United States
172: (Government Auditing Standards); the audit requirements of Title 2 U.S. Code of Federal Regulations
172: Part 200, Uniform Administrative Requirements, Cost Principles, and Audit Requirements for Federal
172: Awards (Uniform Guidance) and the Texas Grant Management Standards (TxGMS). Our responsibilities
172: under those standards, the Uniform Guidance and TxGMS are further described in the Auditor’s
172: Responsibilities for the Audit of Compliance section of our report.
172: We are required to be independent of the District and to meet our other ethical responsibilities, in
172: accordance with relevant ethical requirements relating to our audit. We believe that the audit
172: evidence we have obtained is sufficient and appropriate to provide a basis for our opinion on
172: compliance for each major federal and state program. Our audit does not provide a legal
172: determination of the District’s compliance with the compliance requirements referred to above.
172: Responsibilities of Management for Compliance
172: Management is responsible for compliance with the requirements referred to above and for the design,
172: implementation, and maintenance of effective internal control over compliance with the requirements
172: of laws, statutes, regulations, rules and provisions of contracts or grant agreements applicable to the
172: District’s federal and state programs.
172: Independent Auditor’s Report on Compliance for Each Major Federal and State Program and Report on Internal Control over Compliance Required by the Uniform Guidance and Texas
172: Uniform Grant Management Standards
173: - 156 -
173: The School Board of
173: Houston Independent School District
173: 154
173: Auditor’s Responsibilities for the Audit of Compliance
173: Our objectives are to obtain reasonable assurance about whether material noncompliance with the
173: compliance requirements referred to above occurred, whether due to fraud or error, and express an
173: opinion on the District’s compliance based on our audit. Reasonable assurance is a high level of
173: assurance but is not absolute assurance and therefore is not a guarantee that an audit conducted in
173: accordance with GAAS, Government Auditing Standards, the Uniform Guidance and TxGMS will always
173: detect material noncompliance when it exists. The risk of not detecting material noncompliance
173: resulting from fraud is higher than for that resulting from error, as fraud may involve collusion, forgery,
173: intentional omissions, misrepresentations, or the override of internal control. Noncompliance with the
173: compliance requirements referred to above is considered material, if there is a substantial likelihood
173: that, individually or in the aggregate, it would influence the judgment made by a reasonable user of
173: the report on compliance about the District’s compliance with the requirements of each major federal
173: and state program as a whole.
173: In performing an audit in accordance with GAAS, Government Auditing Standards, the Uniform
173: Guidance and TxGMS, we:
173:  Exercise professional judgment and maintain professional skepticism throughout the audit.
173:  Identify and assess the risks of material noncompliance, whether due to fraud or error, and
173: design and perform audit procedures responsive to those risks. Such procedures include
173: examining, on a test basis, evidence regarding the District’s compliance with the compliance
173: requirements referred to above and performing such other procedures as we considered
173: necessary in the circumstances.
173:  Obtain an understanding of the District’s internal control over compliance relevant to the audit
173: in order to design audit procedures that are appropriate in the circumstances and to test and
173: report on internal control over compliance in accordance with the Uniform Guidance and
173: TxGMS, but not for the purpose of expressing an opinion on the effectiveness of the District’s
173: internal control over compliance. Accordingly, no such opinion is expressed.
173: We are required to communicate with those charged with governance regarding, among other
173: matters, the planned scope and timing of the audit and any significant deficiencies and material
173: weaknesses in internal control over compliance that we identified during the audit.
173: Report on Internal Control over Compliance
173: A deficiency in internal control over compliance exists when the design or operation of a control over
173: compliance does not allow management or employees, in the normal course of performing their
173: assigned functions, to prevent, or detect and correct, noncompliance with a type of compliance
173: requirement of a federal or state program on a timely basis. A material weakness in internal control over
173: compliance is a deficiency, or a combination of deficiencies, in internal control over compliance, such
173: that there is a reasonable possibility that material noncompliance with a type of compliance
173: requirement of a federal or state program will not be prevented, or detected and corrected, on a
173: timely basis. A significant deficiency in internal control over compliance is a deficiency, or a
173: combination of deficiencies, in internal control over compliance with a type of compliance
173: requirement of a federal or state program that is less severe than a material weakness in internal control
173: over compliance, yet important enough to merit attention by those charged with governance.
174: - 157 -
174: The School Board of
174: Houston Independent School District
174: 155
174: Our consideration of internal control over compliance was for the limited purpose described in the
174: Auditor’s Responsibilities for the Audit of Compliance section above and was not designed to identify all
174: deficiencies in internal control over compliance that might be material weaknesses or significant
174: deficiencies in internal control over compliance. Given these limitations, during our audit we did not
174: identify any deficiencies in internal control over compliance that we consider to be material
174: weaknesses, as defined above. However, material weaknesses or significant deficiencies in internal
174: control over compliance may exist that were not identified.
174: Our audit was not designed for the purpose of expressing an opinion on the effectiveness of internal
174: control over compliance. Accordingly, no such opinion is expressed.
174: The purpose of this report on internal control over compliance is solely to describe the scope of our
174: testing of internal control over compliance and the results of that testing based on the requirements of
174: the Uniform Guidance and TxGMS. Accordingly, this report is not suitable for any other purpose.

184: H OUSTON IN D E PE N DE N T S CH O O L DI STR I CT
184: HOUSTON INDEPENDENT SCHOOL DISTRICT
184: Notes to Schedule of Expenditures of Federal and State Awards
184: June 30, 2024
184: 1. The Houston Independent School District (the “District”) utilizes the funds specified in the Texas Education
184: Agency Financial Accountability System Resource Guide. The information in the Schedule of Expenditures
184: of Federal Awards (SEFA) is presented in accordance with the requirements of the Uniform Guidance and
184: the Schedule of Expenditures of State Awards (SESA) is presented in accordance with the requirements of
184: Texas Grant Management Standards.
184: The Special Revenue Fund is used to account for resources restricted to, or designated for, specific purposes
184: by a grantor. Federal and state financial assistance generally is accounted for in a Special Revenue Fund.
184: Generally, unused balances are returned to the grantor at the close of specified project periods.
184: 2. The accounting and financial reporting treatment applied to a fund is determined by its measurement focus.
184: The governmental funds are accounted for using a current-financial-resources measurement focus. All federal
184: and state grant funds are accounted for in the Special Revenue Fund, a component of the governmental
184: funds. With this measurement focus, only current assets and current liabilities, generally, are included on the
184: balance sheet. Operating statements of these funds present increases (i.e., revenues and other financing
184: sources) and decreases (i.e., expenditures and other financing uses) in net current assets.
184: The modified-accrual basis of accounting is used for the governmental funds. This basis of accounting
184: recognizes revenues in the accounting period in which they become both measurable and available and
184: expenditures in the accounting period in which the fund liability is incurred, except that principal and interest
184: on general long-term debt are recognized when due.
184: National School Lunch Program and Summer Food Service Program non-cash commodities are recorded at
184: their estimated market value at the time of donation.
184: Federal and state grant funds are considered to be earned when all eligibility requirements have been met.
184: Any excess of revenues or expenditures is recorded as unearned revenues or accounts receivable,
184: respectively.
184: 3. The period of availability for federal funds for the purpose of liquidating all financial obligations incurred on or
184: before the ending date of the federal project period extended 120 calendar days beyond the federal project
184: period ending date as specified in the terms and conditions (2 CFR section 200.344(b)).
184: 4. The disbursement of funds received under federal grant programs generally requires compliance with terms
184: and conditions specified in the grant agreements and is subject to audit by the grantor agencies. Any
184: disallowed claims resulting from such audits could become a liability of the General Fund. In the opinion of
184: management, such disallowed claims, if any, will not have a material effect on any of the financial statements
184: of the individual fund types or on the overall financial position of the District at June 30, 2024.
184: Total Expenditures of Federal Financial Assistance $ 736,678,395
184: Public Assistance Grants (ALN 97.036) DR-4485 26,325,392
184: COVID-19 Emergency Connectivity Fund (ALN 32.009) 12,399,077
184: Additional federal revenue reported:
184: Interest Subsidy on Build America & Qualified School Construction Bonds 4,432,143
184: School Health and Related Services (SHARS) 3,286,676
184: Total Federal Revenues Reported $ 783,121,683
